is equivalent to either of the following conditions.

(1) There exists a dense set
A\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
such that
h_{n}(v)\to h(v)
for all
v\in A
.

(2) For all compact
C\subset{\operatorname*{Dom}\,}h
not containing a boundary point of
{\operatorname*{Dom}\,}h
, it holds

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{v\in C}|h_{n}(v)-h(v)|=0\;.

Lemma C.11

(Proposition 7.33,
Rockafellar and Wets 2009
)

Let
h_{n}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
,
h:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
be closed and proper. If
h_{n}
has bounded sublevel sets and
h_{n}{\,\overset{\mathrm{epi}}{\longrightarrow}\,}h
, then
\inf_{v}h_{n}(v)\to\inf_{v}h(v)
.

Lemma C.12

(Theorem 7.31,
Rockafellar and Wets 2009
)

Let
h_{n}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
,
h:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
satisfy
h_{n}{\,\overset{\mathrm{epi}}{\longrightarrow}\,}
and
-\infty<\inf h<\infty
. Let
S_{n}(\varepsilon)=\left\{\theta\mid h_{n}(\theta)\leq\inf h_{n}+\varepsilon\right\}
and
S(\varepsilon)=\left\{\theta\mid h(\theta)\leq\inf h+\varepsilon\right\}
. Then
\limsup_{n}S_{n}(\varepsilon)\subset S(\varepsilon)
for all
\varepsilon\geq 0
, and
\limsup_{n}S_{n}(\varepsilon_{n})\subset S(0)
whenever
\varepsilon_{n}\downarrow 0
.

Lemma C.13

(Theorem 5.7,
Shapiro et al. 2021
, Asymptotics of SAA Optimal Value)

Consider the problem

\min_{x\in X}f(x)={\mathbb{E}}[F(x,\xi)]

where
X
is a nonempty closed subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
\xi
is a random vector with probability distribution

P
on a set
\Xi
and
F:X\times\Xi\to{\mathbb{R}}
. Assume the expectation is well-defined, i.e.,
f(x)<\infty
for all
x\in X
. Define the sample average approxiamtion (SAA) problem

\min_{x\in X}f_{N}(x)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}F(x,\xi_{i})

where
\xi_{i}
are i.i.d. copies of the random vector
\xi
. Let
v_{N}
(resp.,
v^{*}
) be the optimal value of the SAA problem (resp., the original problem). Assume the following.

C.13.a

The set
X
is compact.

C.13.b

For some point
x\in X
the expectation
{\mathbb{E}}[F(x,\xi)^{2}]
is finite.

C.13.c

There is a measurable function
C:\Xi\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
such that
{\mathbb{E}}[C(\xi)^{2}]<\infty

and
\left|F(x,\xi)-F\left(x^{\prime},\xi\right)\right|\leq C(\xi)||x-x^{\prime}\|
for all
x,x^{\prime}\in X
and almost every
\xi\in\Xi
.

C.13.d

The function
f
has a unique minimizer
x^{*}
on
X
.

Then

{v}_{N}={f}_{N}(x^{*})+o_{p}(N^{-1/2})\;,\quad\sqrt{N}(v_{N}-v^{*}){\,\overset{{\,d\,\,}}{\rightarrow}\,}{\mathcal{N}}\big(0,{\operatorname{Var}}\big({F(x^{*},\xi)}\big)\big)\;.

Appendix D
Proofs of Main Theorems of LFM

D.1
Proof of Theorem
B.1

Proof D.1

(Proof of
theorem

B.1
)

We show epi-convergence (see
definition

C.7
) of
H_{t}
to
H
.
Epi-convergence is closely related to the question of whether we have convergence of the set of minimizers. In particular, epi-convergence is a suitable notion of convergence under which one can guarantee that the set of minimizers of the sequence of approximate optimization problems converges to the minimizers of the original problem.

To work under the framework of epi-convergence, we extend the definition of
H_{t}
and
H
to the entire Euclidean space as follows.
We extend
\log
to the entire real by defining
\log(x)=-\infty
if
x<0
.
Let

\displaystyle\tilde{F}(\theta,\beta)=\begin{cases}F(\theta,\beta)=\max_{i}v_{i}(\theta)\beta_{i}-{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}b_{i}\log\beta_{i}&\text{if $\beta\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{++}}$}\\
+\infty&\text{else}\end{cases},

and

\displaystyle\tilde{H}(\beta):{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}},\beta\mapsto\begin{cases}H(\beta)&\text{if $\beta\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{++}}$}\\
+\infty&\text{else}\end{cases},\quad\tilde{H}_{t}(\beta):{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}},\beta\mapsto\begin{cases}H_{t}(\beta)&\text{if $\beta\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{++}}$}\\
+\infty&\text{else}\end{cases}.

It is clear that for
\beta\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
it holds
\tilde{H}(\beta)={\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{F}(\theta,\beta)]
and
\tilde{H}_{t}(\beta)=\frac{1}{t}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}\tilde{F}({\theta^{\tau}},\beta)
.
In order to prove the result, we will invoke Lemmas
C.10
,
C.11
, and
C.12
.
To invoke those lemmas, we will need the following four properties that we each prove immediately after stating them.

1.

Check that
\tilde{H}
is closed, proper and convex, and
\tilde{H}_{t}
is closed, proper and convex almost surely. Convexity and properness of the functions
\tilde{H}_{t}
and
\tilde{H}
is obvious.
Recall for a proper convex function, closedness is equivalent to lower semicontinuity
(
Rockafellar 1970
, Page 52)
.
It is obvious that
\tilde{H}_{t}
is continuous and thus closed almost surely.

It remains to verify lower semicontinuity of
\tilde{H}
,
i.e., for all
\beta\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
,
\liminf_{\beta^{\prime}\to\beta}\tilde{H}(\beta^{\prime})\geq\tilde{H}(\beta)
.
For any
\beta\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
, we have that

\tilde{f}(\theta,\beta)=\max_{i}{v_{i}(\theta)}\beta_{i}+\delta_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}}(\beta)\geq 0
, where
\delta_{A}(\beta)=\infty
if
\beta\notin A
and
0
if
\beta\in A
.
With this definition of
\tilde{f}
we have

\tilde{F}(\theta,\beta)=\tilde{f}(\theta,\beta)-{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}b_{i}\log\beta_{i}
.
Applying Fatou’s lemma (for extended real-valued random variables), we get

\liminf_{\beta^{\prime}\to\beta}{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{f}(\theta,\beta^{\prime})]\geq{\mathbb{E}}[\liminf_{\beta^{\prime}\to\beta}\tilde{f}(\theta,\beta^{\prime})]\geq{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{f}(\theta,\beta)]
where in the last step we used lower semicontinuity of
\beta\mapsto\tilde{f}(\theta,\beta)
.
And thus

\displaystyle\liminf_{\beta^{\prime}\to\beta}\tilde{H}(\beta^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\liminf_{\beta^{\prime}\to\beta}{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\tilde{f}(\theta,\beta^{\prime})-{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}b_{i}\log\beta^{\prime}_{i}\bigg]

\displaystyle\geq\liminf_{\beta^{\prime}\to\beta}{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{f}(\theta,\beta^{\prime})]-{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}b_{i}\log\beta_{i}

\displaystyle\geq{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\tilde{f}(\theta,\beta)-{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}b_{i}\log\beta_{i}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\tilde{H}(\beta)

This shows
\tilde{H}
is lower semicontinuous.

2.

Check
\tilde{H}_{t}
pointwise converges to
\tilde{H}
on
{\mathbb{Q}}^{n}
.
Let
{\mathbb{Q}}^{n}
be the set of
n
-dimensional vectors with rational entries.
For a fixed
\beta\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
, define the event
E_{\beta}=\{\lim_{t\to\infty}\tilde{H}_{t}(\beta)=\tilde{H}(\beta)\}
.
Since
{v_{i}(\theta^{\tau})}\leq{\bar{v}}
almost surely by assumption, the strong law of large numbers implies that
{\mathbb{P}}(E_{\beta})=1
. Define

\displaystyle E=\Big\{\lim_{t\to\infty}\tilde{H}_{t}(\beta)=\tilde{H}(\beta),\text{ for all }\beta\in{\mathbb{Q}}^{n}\Big\}=\bigcap_{\beta\in{\mathbb{Q}}^{n}}E_{\beta}.

Then by a union bound we obtain
{\mathbb{P}}(E^{c})={\mathbb{P}}(\bigcup_{\beta\in{\mathbb{Q}}^{n}}E_{\beta}^{c})\leq\sum_{\beta\in{\mathbb{Q}}^{n}}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{\beta}^{c})=0
, implying
E

has measure one.

3.

Check
-\infty<\inf_{\beta}\tilde{H}<\infty
. This is obviously true since valuations are bounded.

4.

Check that for almost every sample path
\omega
,
\tilde{H}_{t}
has bounded sublevel sets (eventually).
By
lemma

C.9
, this property is equivalent to eventual coerciveness of
\tilde{H}_{t}
, i.e., there is a (random)
N
such that for all
t\geq N
, it holds
\lim_{\|\beta\|\to\infty}\tilde{H}_{t}(\beta)=+\infty
.
By
lemma

C.1
, we know for almost every
\omega
, there is a finite constant
N_{\omega}
such that for all
t\geq N_{\omega}
it holds
{\bar{v}_{i}^{t}}\geq 1/2
. Then it holds for this
\omega
, all
t\geq N_{\omega}
, and all
\beta\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
,

\displaystyle\tilde{H}_{t}(\beta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}\max_{i}{v_{i}(\theta^{\tau})}\beta_{i}-{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}b_{i}\log\beta_{i}

\displaystyle\geq\max_{i}({\bar{v}_{i}^{t}}\beta_{i})-{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}b_{i}\log\beta_{i}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\|\beta\|_{\infty}-{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}b_{i}\log\beta_{i}\to+\infty\quad\text{as $\|\beta\|\to\infty$}\;.

This implies
\tilde{H}_{t}
has bounded sublevel sets.

With the above
item

1
and
item

2
we invoke
lemma

C.10
and obtain that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\big(\tilde{H}_{t}(\beta){\,\overset{\mathrm{epi}}{\longrightarrow}\,}\tilde{H}(\beta)\big)=1\;,

(44)

and that the convergence is uniform on any compact set.

The epi-convergence result
eq.

44
along with
item

4
allows us to invoke
lemma

C.11
and obtain

\displaystyle\inf_{\beta\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\tilde{H}_{t}(\beta)\to\inf_{\beta\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\tilde{H}(\beta)\text{ a.s.}

(45)

which also implies
\inf_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{++}}}H_{t}\to\inf_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{++}}H
a.s.

With the epi-convergence result
eq.

44
along with
item

3
we invoke
lemma

C.12
and obtain

\begin{split}\limsup_{t}{\mathcal{B}}^{\gamma}(\epsilon)\subset{\mathcal{B}}^{*}(\epsilon)\text{ for all }\epsilon\geq 0\;,\\
\limsup_{t}{\mathcal{B}}^{\gamma}(\epsilon_{t})\subset{\mathcal{B}}^{*}(0)\text{ for all }\epsilon_{t}\downarrow 0\;.\end{split}

(46)

Putting together.

At this stage all statements in the theorem are direct implications of the above results.

Proof of
B.1.1

Convergence of Nash social welfare follows from
eq.

45
and strong duality, i.e.,
{\mathsf{{NSW}}}^{\gamma}=\inf_{\beta\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{++}}}H_{t}(\beta)+{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}(b_{i}\log b_{i}-b_{i})
and
{\mathsf{{NSW}}}^{*}=\inf_{\beta\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{++}}}H(\beta)+{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}(b_{i}\log b_{i}-b_{i})
.

Proof of
B.1.2

Now we show consistency of the pacing multiplier via

lemma

C.10
and
lemma

C.11
.
Recall the compact set
C=\prod_{i=1}^{n}[{\underaccent{\bar}{\beta}_{i}}/2,2{\bar{\beta}}]=\prod_{i=1}^{n}[b_{i}/2,2]\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
By construction,
\beta^{*}\in C
.
First note that for almost every sample path
\omega
,
1/2\leq{\bar{v}_{i}^{t}}\leq 2
eventually, and thus
\beta^{\gamma}_{i}=b_{i}/u^{\gamma}_{i}\leq b_{i}/(b_{i}{\bar{v}_{i}^{t}})\leq 2
and
\beta^{\gamma}_{i}\geq b_{i}/2
eventually.
So
\beta^{\gamma}\in C
eventually.
Now we can invoke
lemma

C.10
Item (2) to get

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\sup_{\beta\in C}|H_{t}(\beta)-H(\beta)|\to 1\quad\text{a.s.}

(47)

Now we can show that the value of
H
on the sequence
\beta^{\gamma}
converges to the value at
\beta^{*}
:

\displaystyle 0\leq\lim_{t\to\infty}H(\beta^{\gamma})-H(\beta^{*})=\lim_{t\to\infty}[H(\beta^{\gamma})-H_{t}(\beta^{\gamma})]+\lim_{t\to\infty}[H_{t}(\beta^{\gamma})-H(\beta^{*})]=0\;.

Here the first term tends to zero due to (
47
),
and the second term by
eq.

45
.
For any limit point of the sequence
\{\beta^{\gamma}\}_{t}
,
\beta^{\infty}
, by
lower semicontinuity of
H
,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq H(\beta^{\infty})-H(\beta^{*})\leq\liminf_{t\to\infty}H(\beta^{\gamma})-H(\beta^{*})=0\;.

So it holds that
H(\beta^{\infty})=H(\beta^{*})
for all limit points
\beta^{\infty}
. By uniqueness of the optimal solution
\beta^{*}
, we have
\beta^{\gamma}\to\beta^{*}
a.s.

Proof of
B.1.3

Convergence of approximate equilibrium follows from
eq.

46
.

D.2
Proof of Theorem
3.2

Proof D.2

(Proof of
theorem

3.2
)

We abbreviate

C_{\mathsf{{LFM}}}
to
C
, and recall
its definition
C=\prod_{i=1}^{n}[b_{i}/2,2]\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and the normalization

{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}b_{i}=1
and
\nu_{i}=1
for all
i
.
Recall the event
A_{t}=\{\beta^{\gamma}\in C\}
.
By
lemma

C.1
we know that if
t\geq 2{{\bar{v}^{2}}{\log(4n/\eta)}}
then
event
A_{t}
happens
with probability
\geq 1-\eta/2
.
Now the proof proceeds in two steps.

Step 1. A covering number argument.

Let
{\mathcal{B}}^{o}
be an
\epsilon
-covering of the compact set
C
, i.e, for all
\beta\in C
there is a
\beta^{o}(\beta)\in{\mathcal{B}}^{o}
such that
\|\beta-\beta^{o}(\beta)\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon
. It is easy to see that such a set can be chosen with cardinality bounded by
|{\mathcal{B}}^{o}|\leq(2/\epsilon)^{n}
.

Recall
H_{t}
and
H
are
L
-Lipschitz w.r.t.
\ell_{\infty}
-norm on
C
.
Using this fact we get the following uniform concentration bound over the compact set
C
.

\displaystyle\sup_{\beta\in C}|H_{t}(\beta)-H(\beta)|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\beta\in C}\big\{|H_{t}(\beta)-H_{t}(\beta^{o}(\beta))|+|H(\beta)-H(\beta^{o}(\beta))|+|H_{t}(\beta^{o}(\beta))-H(\beta^{o}(\beta))|\big\}

\displaystyle\leq 2({\bar{v}}+2n)\epsilon+\sup_{\beta^{o}\in{\mathcal{B}}^{o}}|H_{t}(\beta^{o})-H(\beta^{o})|\;.

Next we bound the second term in the last expression. For some fixed
\beta\in C
, let
X^{\tau}=\max_{i}{v_{i}(\theta^{\tau})}\beta_{i}
and let its mean be
\mu
. Note
0\leq X^{\tau}\leq{\bar{v}}\|\beta\|_{\infty}\leq 2{\bar{v}}
due to
\beta\in C
. So
X^{\tau}
’s are bounded random variables. By Hoeffding’s inequality we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\big(|H_{t}(\beta)-H(\beta)|\geq\delta\big)={\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\Big|\frac{1}{t}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}X^{\tau}-\mu\Big|\geq\delta\bigg)\leq 2\exp\bigg(-\frac{\delta^{2}t}{2{\bar{v}^{2}}}\bigg)\;.

By a union bound we get

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\sup_{\beta^{o}\in{\mathcal{B}}^{o}}|H_{t}(\beta^{o})-H(\beta^{o})|\geq\delta\bigg)\leq 2|{\mathcal{B}}^{o}|\exp\bigg(-\frac{\delta^{2}t}{2{\bar{v}^{2}}}\bigg)\leq 2\exp\bigg(-\frac{\delta^{2}t}{2{\bar{v}^{2}}}+n\log(2/\epsilon)\bigg)\;.

Define the event

\displaystyle E_{t}=\Big\{\sup_{\beta^{o}\in{\mathcal{B}}^{o}}|H_{t}(\beta^{o})-H(\beta^{o})|\leq\frac{2{\bar{v}}}{\sqrt{t}}\sqrt{\log(4/\eta)+n\log(2/\epsilon)}=:\iota\Big\}\;.

(48)

By setting
2\exp(-{\delta^{2}t}/{(2{\bar{v}^{2}})}+n\log(2/\epsilon))=\eta/2
and solving for
\eta
, we have that
{\mathbb{P}}(E_{t})\geq 1-\eta/2
.

Step 2. Putting together.

Recall the event
A_{t}=\{\beta^{\gamma}\in C\}
.
Now let events
A_{t}
and
E_{t}
hold. Note
{\mathbb{P}}(A_{t}\cap E_{t})\geq 1-\eta
if
t\geq 2{{\bar{v}^{2}}{\log(4n/\eta)}}
. Then

\displaystyle\Big|\sup_{\beta\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{++}}}H_{t}(\beta)-\sup_{\beta\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{++}}}H(\beta)\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\sup_{\beta\in C}H_{t}(\beta)-\sup_{\beta\in C}H(\beta)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\beta\in C}|H_{t}(\beta)-H(\beta)|

\displaystyle\leq 2({\bar{v}}+2n)\epsilon+\iota\;,

(49)

where the first equality is due to event
A_{t}
and the last inequality is due to event
E_{t}
defined in
eq.

48
.
Now we choose the discretization error as
\epsilon=\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}({\bar{v}}+2n)}
.
Then, the expression in
eq.

49
can be upper bounded as follows.

\displaystyle 2({\bar{v}}+2n)\epsilon+\iota

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{t}}+\frac{2{\bar{v}}}{\sqrt{t}}\sqrt{\log(4/\eta)+{n}{\log(2\sqrt{t}({\bar{v}}+2n))}}\;.

This completes the proof.

D.3
Proof of Theorem
3.3

Proof D.3

(Proof of
theorem

3.3
)

The proof idea of this theorem closely follows Section 5.3 of
Shapiro et al. 2021
.

We first need some additional notations. Define the approximate solutions sets of surrogate problems as follows: For a closed set
A\subset{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{++}}
, let

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{A}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\{\beta\in A:H(\beta)\leq\min_{A}H+{\epsilon}\}\;,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}^{\gamma}_{A}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\{\beta\in A:H_{t}(\beta)\leq\min_{A}H_{t}+{\epsilon}\}\;.

In words, they solve the surrogate optimization problems which are defined with a new constraint set
A
. Note that if
\beta^{*}\in A
then
{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{A}(\epsilon)=A\cap{\mathcal{B}}^{*}({\epsilon})
. Recall on the compact set
C
, both
H_{t}
and
H
are
L
-Lipschitz and
\lambda
-strongly convex w.r.t the
\ell_{\infty}
-norm, where
L=({\bar{v}}+2n)
and
\lambda=\underaccent{\bar}{b}/4
.

Let
r=\sup\{H(\beta)-H^{*}:\beta\in C\}
. Then if
\epsilon\geq r
then
C\subset{\mathcal{B}^{*}}(\epsilon)
and the claim is trivial. Now we assume
\epsilon<r
.

Define
a=\min\{2\epsilon,(r+{\epsilon})/2\}
. Note
{\epsilon}<a<r
. Define
S=C\cap{\mathcal{B}^{*}}(a)
. The role of
S
will be evident as follows. We will show that, with high probability, the following chain of inclusions holds

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{C}}(\delta)\overset{(1)}{\subset}{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{S}}(\delta)\overset{(2)}{\subset}{\mathcal{B}^{*}_{S}}(\epsilon)\overset{(3)}{\subset}{\mathcal{B}^{*}_{C}}(\epsilon)\,.

Step 1. Reduction to discretized problems.

We let
S^{\prime}
be a
\nu
-cover of the set
S={\mathcal{B}^{*}}(a)\cap C
.
Let
X=S^{\prime}\cup\{\beta^{*}\}
.
In this part the goal is to show

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\big({\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{C}}(\delta)\subset{\mathcal{B}^{*}_{C}}({\epsilon})\big)\geq{\mathbb{P}}\big({\mathcal{B}}^{\gamma}_{X}(\delta^{\prime})\subset{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{X}(\epsilon^{\prime})\big)

where

\displaystyle\nu=({\epsilon}^{\prime}-\delta^{\prime})/4>0\,,\quad\delta^{\prime}=\delta+L\nu>0\,,\quad\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon-L\nu>0\,.

First, we claim

Claim 1

It holds
{\mathcal{B}}^{\gamma}_{X}(\delta^{\prime})\subset{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{X}(\epsilon^{\prime})\implies{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{S}}(\delta)\overset{}{\subset}{\mathcal{B}^{*}_{S}}(\epsilon)
(Inclusion (2)).

Next, we show

Claim 2

Inclusion (2) implies Inclusion (1):

{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{S}}(\delta)\overset{}{\subset}{\mathcal{B}^{*}_{S}}(\epsilon)\implies{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{C}}(\delta)\overset{}{\subset}{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{S}}(\delta)\,.

Proofs of
1
and
2
are deferred after the proof of
theorem

3.3
. At a high level,
1
uses the covering property of the set
X
.
2
exploits convexity of the problem.

Finally, we show Inclusion (3)
{\mathcal{B}^{*}_{S}}(\epsilon)\overset{}{\subset}{\mathcal{B}^{*}_{C}}(\epsilon)
.
Note that
\beta^{*}
belongs to both
C
and
S
. And thus for any
\beta\in{\mathcal{B}^{*}_{S}}(\epsilon)
, it holds
H(\beta)\leq\min_{X}H+{\epsilon}={H^{*}}+{\epsilon}=\min_{S}H+{\epsilon}\,.
We obtain
\beta\in{\mathcal{B}^{*}_{C}}(\epsilon)
.

To summarize,
1
shows that
{\mathcal{B}}^{\gamma}_{X}(\delta^{\prime})\subset{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{X}(\epsilon^{\prime})
implies Inclusion (2).
Inclusion (3) holds automatically.
By
2
we know Inclusion (2) implies Inclusion (1). So it holds deterministically that

\displaystyle\{{\mathcal{B}}^{\gamma}_{X}(\delta^{\prime})\subset{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{X}(\epsilon^{\prime})\}\subset\{{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{C}}(\delta)\subset{\mathcal{B}^{*}_{C}}({\epsilon})\}\;.

Step 2. Probability of inclusion for discretized problems.

Now we bound the probability
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{B}}^{\gamma}_{X}(\delta^{\prime})\subset{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{X}(\epsilon^{\prime}))
.

For now, we forget the construction
X=S^{\prime}+\{\beta^{*}\}
where
S^{\prime}
is a
\nu
-cover of
S
.
Let
X\subset C
be any discrete set with cardinality
|X|
.

Let
{\beta^{*}_{X}}\in\argmin_{X}H
be a minimizer of
H
over the set
X
. For
\beta\in X
define the random variable
Y^{\tau}_{\beta}=F({\theta^{\tau}},{\beta^{*}_{X}})-F({\theta^{\tau}},\beta)
. Also let
\mu_{\beta}={\mathbb{E}}[Y^{\tau}_{\beta}]
, which is well-defined by the i.i.d. item assumption. Let
D=\sup_{\beta\in X}\|\beta-{\beta^{*}_{X}}\|_{\infty}
.

Consider any
0\leq\delta^{\prime}<\epsilon^{\prime}
. If
X-{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{X}(\epsilon^{\prime})
is empty, then all elements in
X
are
\epsilon^{\prime}
-optimal for the problem
\min_{X}H
. Next assume
X-{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{X}(\epsilon^{\prime})
is not empty. We upper bound the probability of the event
{\mathcal{B}}^{\gamma}_{X}(\delta^{\prime})\not\subset{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{X}(\epsilon^{\prime})
.

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\big({\mathcal{B}}^{\gamma}_{X}(\delta^{\prime})\not\subset{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{X}(\epsilon^{\prime})\big)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}\big(\text{there exists }\beta\in X-{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{X}(\epsilon^{\prime}),\,H_{t}(\beta)\leq H_{t}({\beta^{*}_{X}})+\delta^{\prime}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\beta\in X-{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{X}(\epsilon^{\prime})}{\mathbb{P}}\big(H_{t}(\beta)\leq H_{t}({\beta^{*}_{X}})+\delta^{\prime}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta\in X-{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{X}(\epsilon^{\prime})}{\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\frac{1}{t}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}Y^{\tau}_{\beta}\geq-\delta^{\prime}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\beta\in X-{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{X}(\epsilon^{\prime})}{\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\frac{1}{t}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}Y^{\tau}_{\beta}-\mu_{\beta}\geq{\epsilon}^{\prime}-\delta^{\prime}\bigg)

(A)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\beta\in X-{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{X}(\epsilon^{\prime})}\exp\Big(-\frac{2t({\epsilon}^{\prime}-\delta^{\prime})^{2}}{L_{f}^{2}\|\beta-\beta^{*}\|_{\infty}^{2}}\Big)

(B)

\displaystyle\leq|X|\exp\Big(-\frac{2t({\epsilon}^{\prime}-\delta^{\prime})^{2}}{L_{f}^{2}\|\beta-{\beta^{*}_{X}}\|_{\infty}^{2}}\Big)\,.

(50)

Here in (A) we use the fact that
\mu_{\beta}=H({\beta^{*}_{X}})-H(\beta)>-{\epsilon}^{\prime}
for
\beta\in X-{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{X}(\epsilon^{\prime})
. In (B), using
L_{f}
-Lipschitzness of
f
on the set
C
, we obtain
|Y^{\tau}_{\beta}-\mu_{\beta}|\leq 2L_{f}\|\beta-{\beta^{*}_{X}}\|_{\infty}
and then apply Hoeffding’s inequality for bounded random variables. Setting
eq.

50
equal to
\alpha
and solving for
t
, we have that if

\displaystyle t\geq c^{\prime}\cdot\frac{L_{f}^{2}D^{2}}{({\epsilon}^{\prime}-\delta^{\prime})^{2}}\Big(\log|X|+\log\frac{1}{\alpha}\Big)\,,

(51)

then
{\mathbb{P}}\big({\mathcal{B}}^{\gamma}_{X}(\delta^{\prime})\not\subset{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{X}(\epsilon^{\prime})\big)\leq\alpha
. Note the above derivation applies to any finite set
X\subset S
.

Now we use the construction
X=S^{\prime}+\{\beta^{*}\}
.
Then the cardinality of
X
can be upper bounded by
(4/\nu)^{n}
. Note since
\beta^{*}\in X
it holds
\beta^{*}={\beta^{*}_{X}}
.
We apply the result in
eq.

51
with the following parameters

\displaystyle\nu=({\epsilon}^{\prime}-\delta^{\prime})/(4L)\,,\quad\delta^{\prime}=\delta+L\nu\,,\quad\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon-L\nu\,,\quad\epsilon^{\prime}-\delta^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2}(\epsilon-\delta)\,,

\displaystyle D=\min\{\sqrt{2a/\lambda},2\}\,,\quad|X|\leq\Big(\frac{16L}{{\epsilon}-\delta}\Big)^{n}\,.

We justify the choice of
D
. First,
S\subset C
implies
D\leq 2
.
By the
\lambda
-strong convexity of
H
on
C
: for all
\beta\in X\subset S\subset{\mathcal{B}^{*}}(a)
, it holds

\displaystyle(1/2)\lambda\|\beta-{\beta^{*}_{X}}\|^{2}_{\infty}=(1/2)\lambda\|\beta-\beta^{*}\|^{2}_{\infty}\leq H(\beta)-{H^{*}}\leq a

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle D=\sup_{\beta\in X}\|X-{\beta^{*}_{X}}\|_{\infty}\leq\sqrt{2a/\lambda}\,.

Substituting these quantities into the bound
eq.

51
the expression becomes

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\geq c^{\prime}\cdot\frac{L_{f}^{2}}{({\epsilon}-\delta)^{2}}\cdot\min\bigg\{\frac{2a}{\lambda},4\bigg\}\cdot\bigg(n\log\Big(\frac{16L}{{\epsilon}-\delta}\Big)+\log\frac{1}{\alpha}\bigg)\,.

Here
c^{\prime}
is an absolute constant that changes from line to line.
Moreover, noting that
a\leq 2\epsilon
and
\delta\leq\epsilon/2
implies
a/(\epsilon-\delta)^{2}\leq 8/\epsilon
, we know that if

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\geq c^{\prime}\cdot L_{f}^{2}\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{\lambda\epsilon},\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\bigg\}\cdot\bigg(n\log\Big(\frac{16L}{{\epsilon}-\delta}\Big)+\log\frac{1}{\alpha}\bigg)\;,

(52)

then

{\mathbb{P}}\big({\mathcal{B}}^{\gamma}_{X}(\delta^{\prime})\subset{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{X}(\epsilon^{\prime})\big)\geq 1-\alpha
.
By plugging in
L_{f}={\bar{v}}
,
L=(2n+{\bar{v}})
and
\lambda=\underaccent{\bar}{b}/4
, we know

{\mathbb{P}}\Big({\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{S}}(\delta)\overset{}{\subset}{\mathcal{B}^{*}_{S}}(\epsilon)\Big)\geq 1-\alpha
as long as

\displaystyle t\geq c^{\prime}\cdot{\bar{v}}^{2}\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{\underaccent{\bar}{b}\epsilon},\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\bigg\}\cdot\bigg(n\log\Big(\frac{16(2n+{\bar{v}})}{{\epsilon}-\delta}\Big)+\log\frac{1}{\alpha}\bigg)\;.

Step 3. Putting together.

By
lemma

C.1
,
if
t\geq 2{\bar{v}^{2}}{\log(2n/\alpha)}
then
\beta^{\gamma}\in C
with probability
\geq 1-\alpha
. Under the event
\beta^{\gamma}\in C
, it holds
{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{C}}(\delta)=C\cap{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}}(\delta)
. Since
\beta^{*}\in C
it holds that
{\mathcal{B}^{*}_{C}}({\epsilon})=C\cap{\mathcal{B}^{*}}({\epsilon})
.
Moreover, if
t
satisfies the bound in
eq.

52
, we know Inclusion (2) holds with probability
\geq 1-\alpha
, which then implies Inclusion (1).
So if
t
satisfies the two requirements,
t\geq 2{\bar{v}^{2}}{\log(2n/\alpha)}
and
eq.

52
, then with probability
\geq 1-2\alpha
,

\displaystyle C\cap{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}}(\delta)={\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{C}}(\delta)\subset{\mathcal{B}^{*}_{C}}({\epsilon})=C\cap{\mathcal{B}^{*}}({\epsilon})\,.

Proof D.4

(Proof of
1
)

To see this, for
\beta\in{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{S}}(\delta)
let
\beta^{\prime}\in X
be such that
\|\beta-\beta^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq\nu
. By Lipschitzness of
H_{t}
on
C
, we know

\displaystyle H_{t}(\beta^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq H_{t}(\beta)+L\nu

(Lipschitzness of
H_{t}
)

\displaystyle\leq\min_{S}H_{t}+\delta+L\nu

(
\beta\in{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{S}}(\delta)
)

\displaystyle\leq\min_{X}H_{t}+\delta+L\nu

(
X\subset S
)

\displaystyle=\min_{X}H_{t}+\delta^{\prime}\,.

This implies the membership
\beta^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{X}}(\delta^{\prime})
. Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{X}}(\delta^{\prime})\subset{\mathcal{B}^{*}_{X}}({\epsilon}^{\prime})\subset{\mathcal{B}^{*}_{C}}(\epsilon^{\prime})\,.

Here the first inclusion is simply the assumption that
{\mathcal{B}}^{\gamma}_{X}(\delta^{\prime})\subset{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{X}(\epsilon^{\prime})
. The second inclusion follows by the construction of
X
; since
\beta^{*}\in X
, we know
{\mathcal{B}^{*}_{X}}(\epsilon^{\prime})\subset{\mathcal{B}^{*}_{C}}(\epsilon^{\prime})
and thus
\min_{X}H=\min_{X}H={H^{*}}
. We now obtain

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{B}^{*}_{C}}(\epsilon^{\prime})\,.

Using the Lipschitzness of
H
on
C
, we have for all
\beta\in{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{S}}(\delta)

\displaystyle H(\beta)

\displaystyle\leq H(\beta^{\prime})+L\nu

(Lipschitzness of
H
)

\displaystyle\leq\min_{C}H+\epsilon^{\prime}+L\nu

(
\beta^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{B}^{*}_{C}}(\epsilon^{\prime})
)

\displaystyle=\min_{C}H+\epsilon\,.

So we conclude
\beta\in{\mathcal{B}^{*}_{C}}(\epsilon)
, implying
{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{S}}(\delta)\subset{\mathcal{B}^{*}_{C}}(\epsilon)
. This completes the proof of
1
.

Proof D.5

(Proof of
2
)

This claim relies on convexity of the problem.

Assume, for the sake of contradiction, there exists
{\beta^{\diamond}}\in{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{C}}(\delta)
but
{\beta^{\diamond}}\not\in{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{S}}(\delta)
. The only possibility this can happen is
{\beta^{\diamond}}\in C
but
{\beta^{\diamond}}\not\in S=C\cap{\mathcal{B}^{*}}(a)
. So
{\beta^{\diamond}}\not\in{\mathcal{B}^{*}}(a)
(note
a<r
implies the set
C-{\mathcal{B}^{*}}(a)
is not empty), which by definition means

\displaystyle H({\beta^{\diamond}})-{H^{*}}>a\,.

(53)

Now define

\displaystyle{\bar{\beta}}=\argmin_{\beta\in S}H_{t}(\beta)\in{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{S}}(\delta)\,.

By the assumption
{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{S}}(\delta)\overset{}{\subset}{\mathcal{B}^{*}_{S}}(\epsilon)
, we know
{\bar{\beta}}\in{\mathcal{B}^{*}_{S}}({\epsilon})
and so

\displaystyle H({\bar{\beta}})-{H^{*}}\leq\epsilon\,.

(54)

Next, let
\beta^{c}=c{\bar{\beta}}+(1-c){\beta^{\diamond}}
with
c\in[0,1]
, which is a point lying on the line segment joining the two points
{\bar{\beta}}
and
{\beta^{\diamond}}
. By the optimality of
{\beta^{\diamond}}\in{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{C}}(\delta)
and
{\bar{\beta}}\in C
, we know
H_{t}({\beta^{\diamond}})\leq H({\bar{\beta}})+\delta
.
By convexity of
H_{t}
, we have for all
c\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle H_{t}(\beta^{c})\leq\max\{H_{t}({\bar{\beta}}),H_{t}({\beta^{\diamond}})\}\leq H_{t}({\bar{\beta}})+\delta\,.

(55)

Now consider the map
K:[0,1]\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+},c\mapsto H(\beta^{c})-{H^{*}}
. Since any convex function is continuous on its effective domain
(
Rockafellar 1970
, Corollary 10.1.1)
, we know
H
is continuous. Continuity of
H
implies continuity of
K
.
Note
K(0)=H({\beta^{\diamond}})-{H^{*}}>a
by
eq.

53
and
K(1)=H({\bar{\beta}})-{H^{*}}\leq\epsilon
by
eq.

54
. By intermediate value theorem, there is
c^{*}\in[0,1]
such that
{\epsilon}<H(\beta^{c*})-{H^{*}}<a
. Moreover, by
H(\beta^{c*})-{H^{*}}<a
and
\beta^{c*}\in C
we obtain
\beta^{c*}\in S={\mathcal{B}^{*}}(a)\cap C
. In addition, recalling
H_{t}(\beta^{c*})\leq H_{t}({\bar{\beta}})+\delta
(
eq.

55
), we conclude by definition
\beta^{c*}\in{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{S}}(\delta)
.

At this point we have shown the existence of a point
\beta^{c*}
such that

\displaystyle\beta^{c*}\in{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{S}}(\delta)\,,\quad\beta^{c*}\not\in{\mathcal{B}^{*}}({\epsilon})\,.

This clearly contradicts the assumption
{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}_{S}}(\delta)\overset{}{\subset}{\mathcal{B}^{*}_{S}}(\epsilon)={\mathcal{B}^{*}}(\epsilon)\cap S
.
This completes the proof of
2
.

Proof D.6

(Proof of
corollary

3.4
)

Under the event
\{\beta^{\gamma}\in C\}
, the set
C\cap{\mathcal{B}^{\gamma}}(0)=\{\beta^{\gamma}\}
.
Moreover,
\beta^{\gamma}\in C\cap{\mathcal{B}^{*}}(\epsilon)
implies
H(\beta^{\gamma})\leq H(\beta^{*})+\epsilon
. This completes the proof.

Proof D.7

(Proof of
corollary

3.5
)

Under the event
\{\beta^{\gamma}\in C\}
, we use strong convexity of
H
over
C
w.r.t.
\ell_{2}
-norm and obtain
\frac{\lambda}{2}\|\beta^{\gamma}-\beta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\leq H(\beta^{\gamma})-H(\beta^{*})
where
\lambda=\underaccent{\bar}{b}/4
is the strong-convexity parameter.

For the second claim we use the equality
\beta^{\gamma}_{i}=b_{i}/u^{\gamma}_{i}
and
{\beta^{*}_{i}}=b_{i}/{u^{*}_{i}}
.
For
\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in C
, it holds
|\frac{1}{\beta_{i}}-\frac{1}{\beta^{\prime}_{i}}|\leq\frac{4}{{b_{i}}^{2}}|\beta_{i}-\beta^{\prime}_{i}|
.
And so
\|u^{\gamma}-u^{*}\|_{2}={\sum_{i}}(b_{i})^{2}(\frac{1}{\beta^{\gamma}_{i}}-\frac{1}{{\beta^{*}_{i}}})^{2}\leq{\sum_{i}}\frac{16}{(b_{i})^{2}}|\beta^{\gamma}_{i}-{\beta^{*}_{i}}|^{2}\leq\frac{16}{(\underaccent{\bar}{b})^{2}}\|\beta^{\gamma}-\beta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}
.
So we obtain
\|u^{\gamma}-u^{*}\|_{2}\leq\frac{4}{\underaccent{\bar}{b}}\|\beta^{\gamma}-\beta^{*}\|_{2}
.
We complete the proof.

D.4
Proof of Theorems
3.6
and
3.7

Proof D.8

(Proof of
theorem

3.6
)

By strong duality of EG programs,
{\mathsf{{NSW}}}^{\gamma}-{\mathsf{{NSW}}}^{*}=H_{t}(\beta^{\gamma})-H^{*}(\beta^{*})
.
Denote
H_{t}(\beta^{\gamma})
by
H^{\gamma}
and
H(\beta^{*})
by
H^{*}
.
We aim to apply
lemma

C.13
to our problem. To do this we first introduce surrogate problems

\displaystyle H^{\gamma}_{C}=\min_{\beta\in C}H_{t}(\beta)\;,\quad H^{*}_{C}=\min_{\beta\in C}H(\beta)\;.

Since
\beta^{*}\in C
we know
H^{*}_{C}={H^{*}}
. We write down the decomposition

\displaystyle\sqrt{t}(H^{\gamma}-{H^{*}})=\sqrt{t}(H^{\gamma}-H^{\gamma}_{C})+\sqrt{t}(H^{\gamma}_{C}-H^{*}_{C})\;.

For the first term we show that
\sqrt{t}(H^{\gamma}-H^{\gamma}_{C}){\,\overset{{p}}{\to}\,}0
. Choose any
{\epsilon}>0
, define the event
A^{\epsilon}_{t}=\{\sqrt{t}|H^{\gamma}-H^{\gamma}_{C}|\geq{\epsilon}\}
. By
lemma

C.1
we know that with probability 1,
\beta^{\gamma}\in C
eventually and so
H^{\gamma}-H^{\gamma}_{C}=0
eventually. This implies
{\mathbb{P}}((A^{\epsilon}_{t})^{c}\text{ eventually})=1\Leftrightarrow

{\mathbb{P}}(A^{\epsilon}_{t}\text{ infinitely often})=0
. By Fatou’s lemma,

\limsup_{t\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}}(A^{\epsilon}_{t})\leq{\mathbb{P}}(\limsup_{t\to\infty}A^{\epsilon}_{t})=0.

We conclude for all
\epsilon>0
,
\lim_{t\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{t}|H^{\gamma}-H^{\gamma}_{C}|>\epsilon)=0
.

For the second term, we invoke
lemma

C.13
and obtain
\sqrt{t}(H^{\gamma}_{C}-H^{*}_{C}){\,\overset{{\,d\,\,}}{\rightarrow}\,}{\mathcal{N}}(0,{\operatorname{Var}}[F(\theta,\beta^{*})])
, where we recall
F(\theta,\beta)=\max_{i}\beta_{i}v_{i}(\theta)-{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}b_{i}\log\beta_{i}
.
To do this we verify all hypotheses in
lemma

C.13
.

•

The set
C
is compact and therefore
C.13.a
is satisfied.

•

The function
F
is finite for all
\beta\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{++}}
and thus
C.13.b
holds.

•

The function
F(\cdot,\theta)
is
(2n+{\bar{v}})
-Lipschitz on
C
for all
\theta
, and thus
C.13.c
holds.

•

C.13.d
holds because the function
H
has a unique minimizer over
C
.

Now we calculate the variance term.

\displaystyle{\operatorname{Var}}(F(\theta,\beta^{*}))={\operatorname{Var}}(f(\theta,\beta^{*}))={\operatorname{Var}}(\max_{i}v_{i}(\theta){\beta^{*}_{i}})={\operatorname{Var}}(p^{*}(\theta))

By Slutsky’s theorem, we obtain the claimed result.

Proof D.9

(Proof of Theorem
3.7
)

We verify all the conditions in Theorem 2.1 from
Hjort and Pollard 2011
.
This theorem is handy since it uses convexity and avoids verifying stochastic equicontinuity of certain processes.

Because
H
is
C^{2}
at
\beta^{*}
, there exists a neighborhood
N
of
\beta^{*}

such that
H
is continuously differentiable on
N
. By
theorem

5.1
this implies that
the random variable
{\mathsf{{bidgap}}}(\beta,\cdot)^{-1}
is finite almost surely for each
\beta\in N
. This implies
I(\beta,\theta)
is single valued a.s. for
\beta\in N
.

Define

D(\theta)=\nabla F(\theta,\beta^{*})=\nabla f(\theta,\beta^{*})-\nabla\Psi(\beta^{*})

where we recall the subgradient
\nabla f(\theta,\beta^{*})=e_{i(\beta^{*},\theta)}v_{i(\beta^{*},\theta)}
and
i(\beta^{*},\theta)=\argmax_{i}{\beta^{*}_{i}}v_{i}(\theta)
is the winner of item
\theta
when the pacing multiplier of buyers is
\beta^{*}
.
By optimality of
\beta^{*}
we know
\nabla H(\beta^{*})={\mathbb{E}}[D(\theta)]=0
. Moreover, by twice differentiability of
H
at
\beta^{*}
, the following expansion holds:

\displaystyle H(\beta^{*}+h)-H(\beta^{*})=\frac{1}{2}h^{\top}\big(\nabla^{2}H(\beta^{*})\big)h+o(\|h\|_{2}^{2})\;.

For any
h\to 0
, define

R(\theta)=(F(\theta,\beta^{*}+h)-F(\theta,\beta^{*})-D(\theta)^{\top}h)/\|h\|_{2}

measure the first-order approximation error.
To invoke Theorem 2.1 from
Hjort and Pollard 2011
, we check the following stochastic version of differentiability condition holds

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[R(\theta,h)^{2}]=o(1)\quad\text{as }h\to 0\;.

(56)

By
H
being differentiable at
\beta^{*}
,
we know
R(\theta,h){\,\overset{\mathrm{{a.s.\;}}}{\longrightarrow}\,}0
. Since we assume
\max_{i}\esssup v_{i}(\theta)<\infty
, we know the sequence of random variables
R(\theta,h)
is bounded. We conclude
eq.

56
.

At this stage we have verified all the conditions in Theorem 2.1 from
Hjort and Pollard 2011
. Invoking the theorem we obtain

\displaystyle\sqrt{t}(\beta^{\gamma}-\beta^{*})=-[\nabla^{2}H(\beta^{*})]^{-1}\Bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}D({\theta^{\tau}})\Bigg)+o_{p}(1)\;.

In particular,
\sqrt{t}(\beta^{\gamma}-\beta^{*}){\,\overset{{\,d\,\,}}{\rightarrow}\,}{\mathcal{N}}(0,[\nabla^{2}H(\beta^{*})]^{-1}{\operatorname{Cov}}(D)[\nabla^{2}H(\beta^{*})]^{-1})
.
Finally, note
{\operatorname{Cov}}(D)={\operatorname{Cov}}({\mu^{*}})
.

Proof of Asymptotic Distribution for
\beta
.

This follows from the discussion above.

Proof of Asymptotic Distribution for
u
.
We use the delta method. Take
g(\beta)=[b_{1}/\beta_{1},\dots,b_{n}/\beta_{n}]
. Then the asymptotic variance of
\sqrt{t}(g(\beta^{\gamma})-g(\beta^{*}))
is
\nabla g(\beta^{*})^{\top}\Sigma_{\beta}\nabla g(\beta^{*})
. Note
\nabla g(\beta^{*})
is the diagonal matrix
{\operatorname*{diag}}(\{-b_{i}/{\beta^{*}_{i}}^{2}\})
.
From here we obtain the expression for
\Sigma_{u}
.

D.5
Proof of
theorem

3.8

Proof D.10

(Proof of
theorem

3.8
)

Pacing multiplier
\beta
.
The lower bound result for
\beta
is an immediate application of Theorem 1 from
Duchi and Ruan 2021
.

NSW and utility.
Based on Le Cam’s local asymptotic normality theory
(
Le Cam et al. 2000
)
,
to establish the local asymptotic minimax optimality of a statistical procedure,
one needs to verify two things.
First, the class of perturbed distributions (the class
\{s_{\alpha,g}\}_{\alpha,g}
in our case) satisfies the locally asymptotically normal (LAN) condition
(
Vaart and Wellner 1996
,
Le Cam et al. 2000
)
. This part is completed by Lemma 8.3 from
Duchi and Ruan 2021
since our construction of perturbed supply distributions follows theirs.
Second, one should verify the asymptotic variance of the statistical procedure equals to the minimax optimal variance.

For a given perturbation
{(\alpha,g)}
, we let
p^{*}_{\alpha,g}
and
{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{*}_{\alpha,g}
be the limit FPPE price and revenue under supply distribution
s_{\alpha,g}
.
Let
S_{\alpha,g}(\theta)=\nabla_{\alpha}\log s_{\alpha,g}(\theta)
be the score function.
So
\nabla_{\alpha}s_{\alpha,g}=s_{\alpha,g}S_{\alpha,g}

and
\int S_{\alpha,g}s_{\alpha,g}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta=0
.
Obviously with our parametrization of
s_{\alpha,g}
we have
S_{0,g}(\theta)=g(\theta)
by
eq.

13
.

Let
{\mathsf{{NSW}}}_{\alpha,g}
be the Nash social welfare under supply
s_{\alpha,g}
.
Then
{\mathsf{{NSW}}}_{\alpha,g}=\int F(\theta,\beta^{*}_{\alpha,g})s_{\alpha,g}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta+\text{constant that does not depend on $\alpha$}
.
So

\displaystyle\nabla_{\alpha}{\mathsf{{NSW}}}_{\alpha,g}=\int[\nabla_{\beta}F(\theta,\beta^{*}_{\alpha,g})\nabla_{\alpha}\beta^{*}_{\alpha,g}+F(\theta,\beta^{*}_{\alpha,g})S_{\alpha,g}(\theta)]s_{\alpha,g}(\theta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta=0+\int F(\theta,\beta^{*}_{\alpha,g})g(\theta)s_{\alpha,g}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta,

and
\nabla_{\alpha}{\mathsf{{NSW}}}_{\alpha,g}|_{\alpha=0}=\int F(\theta,\beta^{*})gs\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta
.
Following the argument in
Duchi and Ruan 2021
it holds that
the asymptotic local mimimax risk
\geq{\mathbb{E}}[L({\mathcal{N}}(0,{\operatorname{Cov}}(F(\theta,\beta^{*}))))]={\mathbb{E}}[L({\mathcal{N}}(0,\sigma^{2}_{{\mathsf{{NSW}}}}))]
.

Let
u^{*}_{\alpha,g}
be the utility under supply
s_{\alpha,g}
.
Note
u^{*}_{\alpha,g}=[b_{1}/\beta^{*}_{\alpha,g,1},\dots,b_{n}/\beta^{*}_{\alpha,g,n}]
.
By a perturbation result by Lemma 8.1 and Prop. 1 from
Duchi and Ruan 2021
, under twice differentiability,

\nabla_{\alpha}\beta^{*}_{\alpha,g}|_{\alpha=0}=-{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}{\mathbb{E}}[\nabla F(\theta,\beta^{*})g(\theta)^{\top}]
.
Then
\nabla_{\alpha}u^{*}_{\alpha,g}|_{\alpha=0}={\operatorname*{diag}}(-b_{i}/({\beta^{*}_{i}})^{2})(\nabla_{\alpha}\beta^{*}_{\alpha,g}|_{\alpha=0})=-{\operatorname*{diag}}(-b_{i}/({\beta^{*}_{i}})^{2}){\mathcal{H}}^{-1}{\mathbb{E}}[\nabla F(\theta,\beta^{*})g(\theta)^{\top}]
.
We conclude the asymptotic local mimimax risk
is lower bounded by
{\mathbb{E}}[L({\mathcal{N}}(0,\Sigma_{u}))]

where
\Sigma_{u}={\operatorname*{diag}}(-b_{i}/({\beta^{*}_{i}})^{2}){\mathcal{H}}^{-1}{\mathbb{E}}[\nabla F(\theta,\beta^{*})\nabla F(\theta,\beta^{*})^{\top}]{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}{\operatorname*{diag}}(-b_{i}/({\beta^{*}_{i}})^{2})
.

D.6
Proof of Theorem
3.9

Proof D.11

(Proof of
theorem

3.9
)

Define the functions

\hat{\sigma}^{2}(\beta)=\frac{1}{t}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}(F({\theta^{\tau}},\beta)-H_{t}(\beta))^{2}
and

\sigma^{2}(\beta)={\operatorname{Var}}(F(\theta,\beta))={\mathbb{E}}[(F(\theta,\beta)-H(\beta))^{2}]
.
We will show uniform convergence of
\hat{\sigma}^{2}
to
\sigma^{2}
on
C
, i.e.,
\sup_{\beta\in C}|\hat{\sigma}^{2}-\sigma^{2}|{\,\overset{\mathrm{{a.s.\;}}}{\longrightarrow}\,}0
.
We first rewrite
{\hat{\sigma}^{2}}
as follows

{\hat{\sigma}^{2}}(\beta)={\frac{1}{t}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}\big(F({\theta^{\tau}},\beta)-H(\beta)\big)^{2}}-{(H_{t}(\beta)-H(\beta))^{2}}:={\mathrm{{I}}}(\beta)-{\mathrm{{II}}}(\beta)
.
By Theorem 7.53 of
Shapiro et al. 2021
(a uniform law of large number result for convex random functions), the following uniform convergence results hold

\sup_{\beta\in C}|{\mathrm{{I}}}(\beta)-\sigma^{2}(\beta)|{\,\overset{\mathrm{{a.s.\;}}}{\longrightarrow}\,}0

and
\sup_{\beta\in C}|{\mathrm{{II}}}(\beta)|{\,\overset{\mathrm{{a.s.\;}}}{\longrightarrow}\,}0
.
The above two inequalities imply
\sup_{\beta\in C}|\hat{\sigma}^{2}-\sigma^{2}|{\,\overset{\mathrm{{a.s.\;}}}{\longrightarrow}\,}0
. Note the variance estimator

\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{{\mathsf{{NSW}}}}=\hat{\sigma}^{2}(\beta^{\gamma})

and the asymptotic variance

\sigma^{2}_{{\mathsf{{NSW}}}}=\sigma^{2}(\beta^{*})
.
By
\beta^{\gamma}{\,\overset{\mathrm{{a.s.\;}}}{\longrightarrow}\,}\beta^{*}
we know,

|\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{{\mathsf{{NSW}}}}-\sigma^{2}_{{\mathsf{{NSW}}}}|=|\hat{\sigma}^{2}(\beta^{\gamma})-\sigma^{2}(\beta^{*})|\leq|\hat{\sigma}^{2}(\beta^{\gamma})-\sigma^{2}(\beta^{\gamma})|+|\sigma^{2}(\beta^{\gamma})-\sigma^{2}(\beta^{*})|\to 0\text{ a.s.}
where the first term vanishes by the uniform convergence just established, the second term by continuity of
\sigma^{2}(\cdot)
at
\beta^{*}
.
Now we have shown
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{{\mathsf{{NSW}}}}
is a consistent variance estimator for the asymptotic variance. Then by Slutsky’s theorem we know
{\sqrt{t}{(\hat{\sigma}_{\mathsf{{NSW}}})^{-1}}({\mathsf{{NSW}}}^{\gamma}-{\mathsf{{NSW}}}^{*})}{\,\overset{{\,d\,\,}}{\rightarrow}\,}{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
. This completes the proof of
theorem

3.9
.

\ECHead

Proofs of FPPE Results

Appendix E
Appendix to FPPE

E.1
Scale-Invariance of FPPE

FPPE has some of the same scale-invariance properties as LFM.
In particular, scaling the budget and supply at the same time does not change the market equilibrium. That is, given a positive scalar
\alpha
, if
(\beta,p)
are the equilibrium pacing multiplier and prices in the market
{\textsf{{FPPE}}}(b,v,s,\Theta)
, then

(\beta,p)
are
the equilibrium quantities in the market
{\textsf{{FPPE}}}(\alpha b,v,\alpha s,\Theta)
.
The same scale-invariance holds for the finite FPPE, i.e.,
if
(\beta,p)={\widehat{\textsf{{FPPE}}}}(b,v,\sigma,\gamma)
, then
(\beta,p)={\widehat{\textsf{{FPPE}}}}(\alpha b,v,\alpha\sigma,\gamma)
.
Given the invariance,
we can see that in order for the budget-supply ratio to match the limit market
{\textsf{{FPPE}}}(b,v,s,\Theta)
, the finite market should be configured as either
{\widehat{\textsf{{FPPE}}}}(tb,v,1,\gamma)

or
{\widehat{\textsf{{FPPE}}}}(b,v,1/t,\gamma)
. We will study the latter, and simply refer to it as the finite FPPE.
Unlike LFM, FPPE does not enjoy invariance to valuation scaling, because buyers have a pacing multiplier of at most one in FPPE.

Appendix F
Technical Lemmas for FPPE

Proof F.1

(Proof of
lemma

2.5
)

To show the first equality, note

\displaystyle\nabla H(\beta^{*})

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}[\nabla_{\beta}\max_{i}\beta_{i}{v_{i}(\theta)}]-[b_{1}/(\beta^{*}_{1}),\dots,b_{n}/(\beta^{*}_{n})]^{\top}

\displaystyle={\bar{\mu}^{*}}-[b_{1}/(\beta^{*}_{1}),\dots,b_{n}/(\beta^{*}_{n})]^{\top}=-{\delta^{*}_{i}}\;.

To show the second equality,
note for any twice differentiable first-order homogenous function
\bar{f}:{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}
,
it must hold
\nabla^{2}{\bar{f}}(\beta)\beta=0
.
And we have
\nabla^{2}H(\beta^{*})\beta^{*}={\operatorname*{diag}}(b_{i}/({\beta^{*}_{i}})^{2})\beta^{*}=[b_{1}/\beta^{*}_{1},\dots,b_{n}/\beta^{*}_{n}]^{\top}
.

F.1
A CLT for constrained
M
-estimator

We introduce a CLT result from
Shapiro 1989
that handles
M
-estimation when the true parameter is on the boundary of the constraint set.
Throughout this section, when we refer to assumptions A1, A2, B2, etc, we mean those assumptions in
Shapiro 1989
.

Let
(\Theta,P)
be a probability space. Consider
f:\Theta\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and a set
B\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Let
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{t}
be a sample of independent random variables with values in
\Theta
having the common probability distribution
P
.
Let
\phi(\beta)=Pf(\cdot,\beta)={\mathbb{E}}[f(\theta,\beta)]
, and
\psi_{t}(\beta)=P_{t}f(\cdot,\beta)=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}f(\theta_{i},\beta)
. Let
\beta_{0}
be the unique minimizer of
\phi
over
B
(Assumption A4 in
Shapiro 1989
).
Let
\vartheta_{t}=\inf_{B}\psi_{t}
and
\hat{\beta}
be an optimal solution.

We begin with some blanket assumptions.
Suppose the geometry of
B
at
\beta_{0}
is given by functions
g_{i}(\beta)
(Assumption B1), i.e., there exists a neighborhood
N
such that

B\cap{N}=\left\{\beta\in{N}:g_{i}(\beta)=0,i\in K;g_{i}(\beta)\leq 0,i\in J\right\},

where
K
and
J
are finite index sets and the constraints in
J
are active at
\beta_{0}
, meaning
g_{i}\left(\beta_{0}\right)=0
for all
i\in J
. Assume the functions
g_{i},i\in K\cup J
, are twice continuously differentiable in a neighborhood of
\beta_{0}
(Assumption B2).
Define the Lagrangian function by

l(\beta,\lambda)=\phi(\beta)+\sum_{i\in K\cup J}\lambda_{i}g_{i}(\beta).

Let
\Lambda_{0}
be the set of optimal Lagrange multipliers, i.e.,
\lambda\in\Lambda_{0}
iff

\nabla l\left(\beta_{0},\lambda\right)=0
(assuming differentiability)
and
\lambda_{i}\geq 0,i\in J
.

Lemma F.2

(Theorems 3.1 and 3.2 from
Shapiro 1989
)

Assume there exists a neighborhood
N
of
\beta_{0}
such that the following holds.

F.2.a

Conditions on the objective function
f
and the distribution
P
.

•

(Assumption A1 in the original paper) For almost every
\theta
,
f(\theta,\beta)
is a continuous function of
\beta
, and for all
\beta\in B
,
f(\theta,\beta)
is a measurable function of
\theta
.

•

(Assumption A2) The family
\{f(\theta,\beta)\},\beta\in B
, is uniformly integrable.

•

(Assumption A4) For all
\theta
, there exist a positive constant
K(\theta)
such that

|f(\theta,w)-f(\theta,\beta)|\leq K(\theta)\|w-\beta\|

for all
\beta,w\in{N}
.

•

(Assumption A5) For each fixed
\beta\in{N},f(\theta,\cdot)
is continuously differentiable at
\beta
for almost every
\theta
.

•

(Assumption A6) The family
\{\nabla f(\theta,\beta)\}_{\beta\in{N}}
, is uniformly integrable.

•

(Assumption D) The expectation
{\mathbb{E}}[\left\|\nabla f\left(\theta,\beta_{0}\right)\right\|^{2}]
is finite.

•

(Assumption B4) The function
\phi
is twice continuously differentiable in a neighborhood of
\beta_{0}
.

F.2.b

Conditions on the optimal solution.

•

(Assumption B3) A constraint qualification, the Mangasarian-Fromovitz condition: The gradient vectors
\nabla g_{i}\left(\beta_{0}\right),i\in K
, are linearly independent,
and there exists a vector
w
such that

w^{\top}\nabla g_{i}\left(\beta_{0}\right)=0,i\in K
and
w^{\top}\nabla g_{i}\left(\beta_{0}\right)<0,i\in J.

•

(Assumption B5) Second-order sufficient conditions:
Let
C
be the cone of critical directions

\displaystyle C=\left\{w:w^{\top}\nabla g_{i}\left(\beta_{0}\right)=0,i\in K;w^{\top}\nabla g_{i}\left(\beta_{0}\right)\leq 0,i\in J;w^{\top}\nabla\phi\left(\beta_{0}\right)\leq 0\right\}.

(57)

The assumption requires that for all nonzero
w\in C
,

\max_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{0}}w^{\top}\nabla^{2}l\left(\beta_{0},\lambda\right)w>0,

F.2.c

Stochastic equicontinuity, a modified version of Assumption C1 in the original paper.
For any sequence
\delta_{t}=o(1)
, the variable

\displaystyle\sup_{\beta:\|\beta-\beta_{0}\|\leq\delta_{t}}\frac{\left\|(\nabla\psi_{t}-\nabla\phi)(\beta)-(\nabla\psi_{t}-\nabla\phi)(\beta_{0})\right\|}{t^{-1/2}+\left\|\beta-\beta_{0}\right\|}=o_{p}(1)

(58)

as
t\rightarrow\infty
. Here the supremum is taken over
\beta
such that
\nabla\psi_{t}(\beta)
exists.

Then it holds that
\hat{\beta}{\,\overset{{p}}{\to}\,}\beta_{0}
.
Let

\displaystyle\zeta_{t}=\nabla\psi_{t}\left(\beta_{0}\right)-\nabla\phi\left(\beta_{0}\right),

(59)

and

\displaystyle q(w)=\max_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{0}}\{w^{\top}\nabla^{2}l\left(\beta_{0},\lambda\right)w\}.

(60)

Then

\vartheta_{t}=\inf_{B}\psi_{t}=\psi_{t}\left(\beta_{0}\right)+\min_{w\in C}\{w^{\top}\zeta_{t}+\frac{1}{2}q(w)\}+o_{p}(t^{-1}).

Furthermore, suppose for all
\zeta
the function
w\mapsto w^{\top}\zeta+\frac{1}{2}q(w)
has a unique minimizer
\bar{\omega}(\zeta)
over
C
. Then

\|\hat{\beta}-\beta_{0}-\bar{\omega}(\zeta_{t})\|=o_{p}(t^{-1/2}).

Remark F.3

(The stochastic equicontinuity condition)

By inspecting the proof, the original Assumption C1,

\sup_{\beta\in B\cap{N}}{\left\|\nabla\psi_{t}(\beta)-\nabla\phi(\beta)-\nabla\psi_{t}\left(\beta_{0}\right)+\nabla\phi\left(\beta_{0}\right)\right\|}/[{t^{-1/2}+\left\|\beta-\beta_{0}\right\|}]=o_{p}(1)
, which requires uniform convergence over a fixed neighborhood
N
,
can be relaxed to the uniform convergence in a shrinking neighborhood of
\beta_{0}
.
The shrinking neighborhood condition is in fact
standard, see, e.g.,
Pakes and Pollard 1989
,
Newey and McFadden 1994
.

Remark F.4

The limit distribution of the minimizer is characterized by three objects: the limit distribution of
\zeta_{t}
defined in
eq.

59
, the critical cone
C
defined in
eq.

57
and the piecewise quadratic function
q
defined in
eq.

60
.

Hessian matrix estimation at the optimum
\beta_{0}
can be done via the numerical difference method.

Lemma F.5

(Hessian estimation via numerical difference)

This lemma is adapted from Theorem 7.4 from
Newey and McFadden 1994

Recall
\phi(\beta)=Pf(\cdot,\beta)
,
\psi_{t}(\beta)=P_{t}f(\cdot,\beta)
and
\zeta_{t}=\nabla\psi_{t}(\beta_{0})-\nabla\phi(\beta_{0})
. We are interested in the Hessian matrix
H=\nabla^{2}\phi(\beta_{0})
.
Let
\beta_{0}
be any point and let
\hat{\beta}
be an estimate of
\beta_{0}
.
Assume

F.5.a

\hat{\beta}-\beta_{0}=O_{p}(t^{-1/2})
;

F.5.b

\phi
is twice differentiable at
\beta_{0}
with non-singular Hessian matrix
H
;

F.5.c

\sqrt{t}\zeta_{t}{\,\overset{{\,d\,\,}}{\rightarrow}\,}N(0,\Omega)
for some matrix
\Omega
;

F.5.d

for any positive sequence
\delta_{t}=o(1)
,
the stochastic equicontinuity condition
eq.

58
holds.

Suppose
\varepsilon_{t}\to 0
and
\varepsilon_{t}\sqrt{t}\to\infty
. Then
\hat{H}{\,\overset{{p}}{\to}\,}H
, where
\hat{H}
is the numerical difference estimator whose
(i,j)
-th entry is

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{ij}=

\displaystyle{[\psi_{t}(\hat{\beta}+e_{i}\varepsilon_{t}+e_{j}\varepsilon_{t})-\psi_{t}(\hat{\beta}-e_{i}\varepsilon_{t}+{e}_{j}\varepsilon_{t})-\psi_{t}(\hat{\beta}+e_{i}\varepsilon_{t}-e_{j}\varepsilon_{t}).}

\displaystyle+\psi_{t}(\hat{\beta}-e_{i}\varepsilon_{t}-e_{j}\varepsilon_{t})]/4\varepsilon_{t}^{2}.

Proof F.6

(Proof of
lemma

F.5
)

We provide a proof sketch following Theorem 7.4 from
Newey and McFadden 1994
and
Lemma 3.3 in
Shapiro 1989
.
By
F.5.a
and
t^{-1/2}=o(\varepsilon_{t})
we know for any vector
a\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
, it holds
\|\hat{\beta}+\varepsilon_{t}a-\beta_{0}\|=O_{p}(\varepsilon_{t})
.
Let
\beta=\hat{\beta}+a\varepsilon_{t}
.
By a mean value theorem for locally Lipschitz functions (see
Clarke 1990
; the lemma is also used in the proof of Lemma 3.3 in
Shapiro 1989
),
there is a (sample-path dependent)
\beta^{\prime}
on the segment joining
\beta
and
\beta_{0}
such that

(\psi_{t}-\phi)(\beta)-(\psi_{t}-\phi)(\beta_{0})=(\zeta^{*}_{t})^{\top}(\beta-\beta_{0}).

for some
\zeta^{*}_{t}\in\partial\psi_{t}(\beta^{\prime})-\nabla\phi(\beta^{\prime})
.
Then

\displaystyle|(\psi_{t}-\phi)(\beta)-(\psi_{t}-\phi)(\beta_{0})|

\displaystyle\leq\|\zeta_{t}\|\|\beta-\beta_{0}\|+\|\zeta^{*}_{t}-\zeta_{t}\|\|\beta-\beta_{0}\|

\displaystyle=\|\zeta_{t}\|\|\beta-\beta_{0}\|+o_{p}(t^{-1/2}+\|\beta^{\prime}-\beta_{0}\|)\|\beta-\beta_{0}\|

(by
F.5.d
)

\displaystyle=O_{p}(t^{-1/2})O_{p}(\varepsilon_{t})+o_{p}(t^{-1/2}+O_{p}(\varepsilon_{t}))O_{p}(\varepsilon_{t})

(by
F.5.c
)

\displaystyle=o_{p}(\varepsilon_{t}^{2})

(61)

Next by
F.5.b
we have a quadratic expansion

\displaystyle\phi(\beta)-\phi(\beta_{0})-\nabla\phi(\beta_{0})^{\top}(\beta-\beta_{0})-\frac{1}{2}(\beta-\beta_{0})^{\top}H(\beta-\beta_{0})=o_{p}(\varepsilon_{t}^{2}).

(62)

Let
a_{\pm\pm}=\pm e_{i}\varepsilon_{t}\pm e_{j}\varepsilon_{t}
,
\hat{\beta}_{\pm\pm}=\hat{\beta}+a_{\pm\pm}
and
d_{\pm\pm}=\hat{\beta}_{\pm\pm}-\beta_{0}
.
Then
d_{\pm\pm}=O_{p}(\varepsilon_{t})
and
d_{\pm\pm}=a_{\pm\pm}+o_{p}(\varepsilon_{t})
.
Applying the above bounds with
\beta\leftarrow\hat{\beta}_{\pm\pm}
, recalling the definition of
\hat{H}_{ij}
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{ij}

\displaystyle=[\psi_{t}(\hat{\beta}_{++})-\psi_{t}(\hat{\beta}_{-+})-\psi_{t}(\hat{\beta}_{+-})+\psi_{t}(\hat{\beta}_{--})]/(4\varepsilon_{t}^{2})

\displaystyle=[\phi(\hat{\beta}_{++})-\phi(\hat{\beta}_{-+})-\phi(\hat{\beta}_{+-})+\phi(\hat{\beta}_{--})+o_{p}(\varepsilon_{t}^{2})]/(4\varepsilon_{t}^{2})

(by
eq.

61
)

\displaystyle=[\nabla\phi(\beta_{0})^{\top}(d_{++}-d_{-+}-d_{+-}+d_{--})

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}(d_{++}^{\top}Hd_{++}-d_{+-}^{\top}Hd_{+-}-d_{-+}^{\top}Hd_{-+}+d_{--}^{\top}Hd_{--})+o_{p}(\varepsilon_{t}^{2})]/(4\varepsilon_{t}^{2})

(by
eq.

62
)

\displaystyle=[0+\frac{1}{2}(a_{++}^{\top}Ha_{++}-a_{-+}^{\top}Ha_{-+}-a_{+-}^{\top}Ha_{+-}+a_{--}^{\top}Ha_{--})+o_{p}(\varepsilon_{t}^{2})]/(4\varepsilon_{t}^{2})

\displaystyle=[4\varepsilon_{t}^{2}H_{ij}+o_{p}(\varepsilon_{t}^{2})]/(4\varepsilon_{t}^{2})

\displaystyle=H_{ij}+\frac{o_{p}(\varepsilon_{t}^{2})}{\varepsilon_{t}^{2}}=H_{ij}+o_{p}(1).

In the above we use
d_{++}^{\top}Hd_{++}=(d_{++}-a_{++})^{\top}Hd_{++}+(d_{++}-a_{++})^{\top}Ha_{++}+a_{++}^{\top}Ha_{++}=o_{p}(\varepsilon_{t}^{2})+a_{++}^{\top}Ha_{++}
, and similarly for other terms.
This completes the proof of
lemma

F.5
.

The original conditions for
lemma

F.5
in
Newey and McFadden 1994

require the true parameter
\beta_{0}
to lie in the interior of
B
. However, this condition is only used to derive the bound
\hat{\beta}-\beta_{0}=O_{p}(t^{-1/2})
, which is assumed in our adapted version.

F.2
Stochastic equicontinuity and VC-subgraph function classes

Next we review classical results from the empirical process literature
(
Vaart and Wellner 1996
,
Giné and Nickl 2021
)
.

We begin with the notions of Donsker function class and stochastic equicontinuity.

Let
(\Theta,P)
be a probability space.
Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a class of measurable functions of finite second moment.
The class
{\mathcal{F}}
is called
P
-Donsker if
a certain central limit theorem holds for the class of random variables
\{\sqrt{t}(P_{t}-P)f:f\in{\mathcal{F}}\}
,
where

P_{t}f=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{t}f(X_{i})
where
X_{i}
’s are i.i.d. draws from
P
.
Because Donskerness will be used as an intermediate step that we will not actually need to show directly or utilize directly,
we refer the reader to Definition 3.7.29 from
Giné and Nickl 2021
for a precise definition.

Lemma F.7

(Donskerness
\Leftrightarrow
stochastic equicontinuity)

Let
d_{P}^{2}(f,g)=P(f-g)^{2}-(P(f-g))^{2}
and consider the pseudo-metric space
\left(\mathcal{F},d_{P}\right)
.
Assume
{\mathcal{F}}
satisfies the condition
\sup_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}}|f(x)-Pf|<\infty
for all
x\in\Theta
.
Then the following are equivalent

•

{\mathcal{F}}
is
P
-Donsker.

•

\left(\mathcal{F},d_{P}\right)
is totally bounded, and stochastic equicontinuity under the L2 function norm holds, i.e., for any
\delta_{t}=o(1)
,

\sup_{(f,g)\in[\delta_{t}]_{L^{2}}}|\sqrt{t}\left(P_{t}-P\right)(f-g)|=o_{p}(1)

as
t\to\infty
,
where
[\delta_{t}]_{L^{2}}=\{(f,g):f,g\in\mathcal{F},d_{P}(f,g)\leq\delta_{t}\}
.

See Theorem 3.7.31 from
Giné and Nickl 2021
.

lemma

F.7
reduces the problem of showing stochastic equicontinuity under the L2 function norm to showing Donskerness.
In order to show Donskerness, we will show that our function class is
VC-subgraph
, which implies Donskerness.
At the end, we will connect stochastic equicontinuity under the L2 function norm to the stochastic equicontinuity that we need (see
lemma

F.14
).

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a class of subsets of a set
\Theta
. Let
A\subseteq\Theta
be a finite set. We say that
\mathcal{C}

shatters

A
if every subset of
A
is the intersection of
A
with some set
C\in\mathcal{C}
. The subgraph of a real function
f
on
\Theta
is the set

G_{f}=\{(s,t):s\in\Theta,t\in\mathbb{R},t\leq f(s)\}.

Definition F.8

(VC-subgraph function classes)

A collection of sets
\mathcal{C}
is a Vapnik-Červonenkis class
(\mathcal{C}
is
VC)
if there exists
k<\infty
such that
\mathcal{C}
does not shatter any subsets of
\Theta
of cardinality
k
.
A class of functions
\mathcal{F}
is
VC
-subgraph if the class of sets
\mathcal{C}=\left\{G_{f}:f\in\mathcal{F}\right\}
is
VC
.
See Definition 3.6.1 and 3.6.8 from
Giné and Nickl 2021

Lemma F.9

(VC subgraph + envelop square integrability
\implies
Donskerness)

If
{\mathcal{F}}
is VC-subgraph, and there exists a measurable
F
such that
f\leq F
for all
f\in{\mathcal{F}}
with

PF^{2}<\infty
, then
{\mathcal{F}}
is
P
-Donsker.
See Theorem 3.7.37 from
Giné and Nickl 2021

Since VC-subgraph implies Donskerness which is equivalent to stochastic equicontinuity, our problem reduces to showing the VC-subgraph property. The following lemmas show how to construct complex VC-subgraph function classes from simpler ones, and will be used in our proof.

Lemma F.10

(Preservation of VC class of sets, Lemma 2.6.17 from
Vaart and Wellner 1996
)

If
{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}
are VC classes of sets. Then
{\mathcal{C}}\cap{\mathcal{D}}=\{C\cap D:C\in{\mathcal{C}},D\in{\mathcal{D}}\}
and
{\mathcal{C}}^{c}=\{C^{c}:C\in{\mathcal{C}}\}
are VC.

Lemma F.11

(Preservation of VC-subgraph function classes)

Let
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
be
VC
-subgraph classes of functions on a set
\Theta
and
g:\Theta\mapsto\mathbb{R}
be a fixed function. Then

\mathcal{F}\vee\mathcal{G}=\{f\vee g:f\in\mathcal{F},g\in\mathcal{G}\}
,

\mathcal{F}+g=\{f+g:f\in\mathcal{F}\}
,

\mathcal{F}\circ\phi=\{f\circ\phi:f\in\mathcal{F}\}
is VC-subgraph for fixed
\phi:{\mathcal{X}}\to\Theta
,
and
\mathcal{F}\cdot g=\{fg:f\in\mathcal{F}\}
are VC-subgraph.
See Lemma 2.6.18 from
Vaart and Wellner 1996

Lemma F.12

(Problem 9 Section 2.6 from
Vaart and Wellner 1996
)

If a collection of sets
\mathcal{C}
is a VC-class, then the collection of indicators of sets in
\mathcal{C}
is a VC-subgraph class of the same index.

In general, the VC-subgraph property is not preserved by multiplication, whereas Donskerness is. Thus, our proof will use the VC-subgraph property up until a final step where we need to invoke multiplication, which will instead be applied on the Donskerness property.

Lemma F.13

(Corollary 9.32 from
Kosorok 2008
)

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
and
{\mathcal{G}}
be Donsker,
then
{\mathcal{F}}\cdot{\mathcal{G}}
is Donsker if both
{\mathcal{F}}
and
{\mathcal{G}}
are uniformly bounded.

For parametric function classes, if the parametrization is continuous in
a certain sense, then
stochastic equicontinuity holds w.r.t. the norm in the parameter space.

Lemma F.14

(From
L^{2}
-norm to parameter norm)

Suppose the function class
{\mathcal{F}}=\{f(\cdot,\beta),\beta\in B\}
,
B\subset{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
, is
P
-Donsker, with an envelope
F
such that
PF^{2}<\infty
. Suppose
\int\left[f(\cdot,\beta)-f\left(\cdot,\beta_{0}\right)\right]^{2}dP\rightarrow 0
as
\beta\rightarrow\beta_{0}
. Then for any positive sequence
\delta_{t}=o(1)
, it holds

\displaystyle\sup_{\beta:\|\beta-\beta_{0}\|<\delta_{t}}|\sqrt{t}\left(P_{t}-P\right)(f(\cdot,\beta)-f\left(\cdot,\beta_{0}\right))|=o_{p}(1).

(63)

See Lemma 2.17 from
Pakes and Pollard 1989
; see also Lemma 1 from
Chen et al. 2003

Lemma F.15

(
Andrews 1994
)

If for any
\delta_{t}=o(1)

eq.

63
holds,
then for any random elements
\beta_{t}
such that
\|\beta_{t}-\beta_{0}\|_{2}=o_{p}(1)
, it holds

\sqrt{t}(P_{t}-P)(f(\cdot,\beta_{t})-f(\cdot,\beta_{0}))=o_{p}(1).

Lemma F.16

Given any
n
fixed functions
v_{i}:\Theta\to{\mathbb{R}}
,
i\in[n]
,
the following function classes

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\{\theta\mapsto\max_{i}\{\beta_{1}v_{1}(\theta),\dots,\beta_{n}v_{n}(\theta)\}:\beta\in B\}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\{\theta\mapsto[D_{f,1},\dots,D_{f,n}](\theta,\beta):\beta\in B\}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{3}

\displaystyle=\{\theta\mapsto\max_{i}(\beta_{i}{v_{i}(\theta)}){\mathds{1}}\{\max_{i\in A}\beta_{i}{v_{i}(\theta)}=\max_{i\in[n]}\beta_{i}{v_{i}(\theta)}\}:\beta\in B\}

are VC-subgraph and Donsker.
Here
D_{f,i}(\theta,\beta)=v_{i}(\theta)\prod_{k=1}^{n}{\mathds{1}}(\beta_{i}{v_{i}(\theta)}\geq\beta_{k}v_{k}(\theta))
and
B=[0,1]^{n}
, and
A
is a nonempty subset of
[n]
.

Proof F.17

(Proof of
lemma

F.16
)

We show
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
is VC-subgraph.
For each
i
, the class

\{\theta\mapsto v_{i}(\theta)\beta_{i}:\beta_{i}\in[0,1]\}
is VC-subgraph (Proposition 4.20 from
Wainwright 2019
, and Example 19.17 from
Van der Vaart 2000
).
By the fact the VC-subgraph function classes are preserved by pairwise maximum (
lemma

F.11
), we know
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
is VC-subgraph.
Moreover, the required envelope condition holds since
\esssup_{\theta}f\leq{\bar{v}}
for all
f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
, so
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
is Donsker by
lemma

F.9
.

We now show
{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
is VC-subgraph.
For a vector-valued function class, we say it is VC-subgraph if each coordinate is VC-subgraph.
First, the class of sets
\{\{v\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}:\beta_{i}v_{i}\geq\beta_{k}v_{k}\}\subset{\mathbb{R}^{n}}:\beta\in B\}
is VC, for all
k\neq i
.
By
lemma

F.10
, we know
the class of sets
\{\{v\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}:\beta_{i}v_{i}\geq\beta_{k}v_{k},\forall k\neq i\}\subset{\mathbb{R}^{n}}:\beta\in B\}
is VC.
By
lemma

F.12
, we obtain that the class

\{\theta\mapsto\prod_{k=1}^{n}{\mathds{1}}(\beta_{i}{v_{i}(\theta)}\geq\beta_{k}v_{k}(\theta)):\beta\in B\}

is VC-subgraph.
Finally, multiplying all functions by a fixed function preserves VC-subgraph classes (
lemma

F.11
), and so

\{\theta\mapsto v_{i}(\theta)\prod_{k=1}^{n}{\mathds{1}}(\beta_{i}{v_{i}(\theta)}\geq\beta_{k}v_{k}(\theta)):\beta\in B\}
is VC-subgraph.
Repeat the argument for each coordinate, and we obtain that
{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
is VC-subgraph.
Moreover, the required envelope condition holds since
\esssup_{\theta}\|D_{f}(\theta,\beta)\|_{2}\leq n{\bar{v}}
for all
D_{f}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
, and so
{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
is Donsker by
lemma

F.9
. We conclude the proof of
lemma

F.16
.

Finally, to see
{\mathcal{F}}_{3}
is VC-subgraph and Donsker, rewrite its functions as

{\mathds{1}}\{\max_{A}\beta_{i}{v_{i}(\theta)}=\max_{[n]}\beta_{i}{v_{i}(\theta)}\}=\prod_{k=1}^{n}(1-\prod_{i\in A}{\mathds{1}}(\beta_{i}{v_{i}(\theta)}<\beta_{k}v_{k}(\theta)))

and apply similar arguments as above.

Appendix G
Proofs for Main Theorems in FPPE

G.1
Proof of
theorem

4.1

The convergence
\beta^{\gamma}{\,\overset{\mathrm{{a.s.\;}}}{\longrightarrow}\,}\beta^{*}
follows the same proof as
theorem

B.1
and is ommitted.
To show almost sure convergence of revenue, we note

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{1}{t}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}{p^{\tau}}-\int_{\Theta}p^{*}(\theta)s(\theta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{t}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}|\max_{i}\{{v_{i}(\theta^{\tau})}\beta^{\gamma}_{i}\}-\max_{i}\{{v_{i}(\theta^{\tau})}\beta^{*}_{i}\}|+\Big|\frac{1}{t}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}\max_{i}\{{v_{i}(\theta^{\tau})}\beta^{*}_{i}\}-\int_{\Theta}p^{*}(\theta)s(\theta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\Big|

\displaystyle\leq{\bar{v}}\|\beta^{\gamma}-\beta^{*}\|_{\infty}+\Big|\frac{1}{t}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}\max_{i}\{{v_{i}(\theta^{\tau})}\beta^{*}_{i}\}-\int_{\Theta}p^{*}(\theta)s(\theta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\Big|{\,\overset{\mathrm{{a.s.\;}}}{\longrightarrow}\,}0\;.

Here the first term converges to zero a.s. by
\beta^{\gamma}{\,\overset{\mathrm{{a.s.\;}}}{\longrightarrow}\,}\beta^{*}
, and the second term converges to
0
a.s. by strong law of large numbers and noting
{\mathbb{E}}[\max_{i}\{v_{i}(\theta)\beta^{*}_{i}\}]={\mathbb{E}}[p^{*}(\theta)]={\mathsf{{REV}}}^{*}
.

G.2
Proof of
theorem

4.2

Proof G.1

(Proof of
theorem

4.2
)

First, by
Gao and Kroer 2022
, there is a natural lower bound for the equilibrium pacing multipliers.
To lower bound
{\beta^{*}_{i}}
,
note

{\beta^{*}_{i}}=b_{i}/{u^{*}_{i}}=b_{i}/({\delta^{*}_{i}}+\mu^{*}_{i})\geq b_{i}/(b_{i}+\int v_{i}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta)=b_{i}/(b_{i}+\nu_{i})
.
Then
b_{i}/(b_{i}+\nu_{i})\leq{\beta^{*}_{i}}\leq 1
.
Define the set

\displaystyle C_{\textsf{{FPPE}}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bigg[\frac{b_{i}}{2\nu_{i}+b_{i}},1\bigg]\;.

Clearly we have
\beta^{*}\in C_{\textsf{{FPPE}}}
. Furthermore, for
t
large enough
\beta^{\gamma}\in C_{\textsf{{FPPE}}}
with high probability.
To see this, if
t
satisfies
t\geq 2({\bar{v}}/\min_{i}{\nu_{i}})^{2}\log(2n/\eta)
, then
\frac{1}{t}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}{v_{i}(\theta^{\tau})}\leq 2{\mathbb{E}}[v_{i}(\theta)]
for all
i
with probability
\geq 1-\eta
. By a bound on
\beta^{\gamma}
in the QME

\beta^{\gamma}_{i}\geq\frac{b_{i}}{b_{i}+\frac{1}{t}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}{v_{i}(\theta^{\tau})}}

(see Section 6 in
Gao and Kroer 2022
), we obtain
\beta^{\gamma}_{i}\geq\frac{b_{i}}{b_{i}+2\nu_{i}}
(recall
\nu_{i}={\mathbb{E}}[v_{i}(\theta)]
).

Let
L_{\textsf{{FPPE}}}
and
\lambda_{\textsf{{FPPE}}}
be the Lipschitz constant and strong convexity constants of
H
and
H_{t}
w.r.t
\ell_{\infty}
-norm on
C_{\textsf{{FPPE}}}
.
We estimate
L_{\textsf{{FPPE}}}
and
\lambda_{\textsf{{FPPE}}}
. On
C_{\textsf{{FPPE}}}
, the minimum eigenvalue of
\nabla^{2}\Psi(\beta)={\operatorname*{diag}}\{\frac{b_{i}}{(\beta_{i})^{2}}\}
can be lower bounded by
\underaccent{\bar}{b}
. So we conclude
\lambda_{\textsf{{FPPE}}}=\underaccent{\bar}{b}
.
And the Lipschitzness constant can be seen by the following. For
\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in C_{\textsf{{FPPE}}}
,

\displaystyle|H_{t}(\beta)-H_{t}(\beta^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{t}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}\big|\max_{i}\{{v_{i}(\theta^{\tau})}\beta_{i}\}-\max_{i}\{{v_{i}(\theta^{\tau})}\beta_{i}^{\prime}\}\big|+{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}b_{i}\big|\log\beta_{i}-\log\beta_{i}^{\prime}\big|

\displaystyle\leq{\bar{v}}\|\beta-\beta^{\prime}\|_{\infty}+{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}b_{i}\cdot\frac{1}{b_{i}/(2{\nu_{i}}+b_{i})}|\beta_{i}-\beta_{i}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\leq\big({\bar{v}}+2{\bar{\nu}}n+1\big)\|\beta-\beta^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\;,

where we used
{\sum_{i}}b_{i}=1
.
Similar argument shows that
H
is also
({\bar{v}}+2{\bar{\nu}}n+1)
-Lipschitz on
C_{\textsf{{FPPE}}}
.
We conclude
L_{\textsf{{FPPE}}}=({\bar{v}}+2{\bar{\nu}}n+1)
.

To obtain the convergence rate, we simply repeat the proof of
theorem

3.3
.
We obtain from
eq.

52
that with probability
\geq 1-2\alpha
,
there exists a constant
c^{\prime}
such that
as long as

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\geq c^{\prime}\cdot L_{\textsf{{FPPE}}}^{2}\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{\lambda_{\textsf{{FPPE}}}\epsilon},\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\bigg\}\cdot\bigg(n\log\Big(\frac{16L_{\textsf{{FPPE}}}}{{\epsilon}-\delta}\Big)+\log\frac{1}{\alpha}\bigg)\;,

(64)

it holds
|H(\beta^{\gamma})-H(\beta^{*})|<\epsilon
and that
\beta^{\gamma}\in C_{\textsf{{FPPE}}}
(see
corollary

3.4
).
Now
eq.

64
shows that for
\epsilon<\underaccent{\bar}{b}

(so that the
1/(\lambda_{\textsf{{FPPE}}}\epsilon)
term in the min becomes dominant) we have

\displaystyle|H(\beta^{\gamma})-H(\beta^{*})|=\tilde{O}_{p}\bigg(\frac{n\big({\bar{v}}+2{\bar{\nu}}n+1\big)^{2}}{\underaccent{\bar}{b}t}\bigg)\;,

where we use
\tilde{O}_{p}
to ignore logarithmic factors of
t
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\|\beta^{\gamma}-\beta^{*}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\beta^{\gamma}-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{2|H(\beta^{\gamma})-H(\beta^{*})|/\lambda_{\textsf{{FPPE}}}}=\tilde{O}_{p}\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{n}\big({\bar{v}}+2{\bar{\nu}}n+1\big)}{\underaccent{\bar}{b}\sqrt{t}}\bigg)\;.

From here we obtain

\displaystyle|{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{\gamma}-{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{*}|

\displaystyle\leq{\bar{v}}\|\beta^{\gamma}-\beta^{*}\|_{\infty}+\Big|\frac{1}{t}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}\max_{i}\{{v_{i}(\theta^{\tau})}\beta^{*}_{i}\}-\int_{\Theta}p^{*}(\theta)s(\theta){\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta}\Big|

\displaystyle=\tilde{O}_{p}\bigg(\frac{{\bar{v}}\sqrt{n}\big({\bar{v}}+2{\bar{\nu}}n+1\big)}{\underaccent{\bar}{b}\sqrt{t}}\bigg)+O_{p}\bigg(\frac{{\bar{v}}}{\sqrt{t}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\tilde{O}_{p}\bigg(\frac{{\bar{v}}\sqrt{n}\big({\bar{v}}+2{\bar{\nu}}n+1\big)}{\underaccent{\bar}{b}\sqrt{t}}\bigg)\;.

We conclude

|{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{\gamma}-{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{*}|=\tilde{O}_{p}\Big(\frac{{\bar{v}}\sqrt{n}({\bar{v}}+2{\bar{\nu}}n+1)}{\underaccent{\bar}{b}\sqrt{t}}\Big)
. This completes the proof of
theorem

4.2
.

G.3
Proof of
theorem

4.3

Proof G.2

(Proof of
theorem

4.3
)

The proof of
theorem

4.3
proceeds by showing that FPPE satisfy a set of regularity conditions that are sufficient for asymptotic normality
(
Shapiro 1989
, Theorem 3.3)
; the conditions are stated in
lemma

F.2
in the appendix. Maybe the hardest condition to verify is the so called stochastic equicontinuity condition (
F.2.c
), which we establish with tools from the empirical process literature.
In particular, we show that the class of functions, parameterized by a pacing multiplier vector
\beta
, that map each item to its corresponding first-price auction allocation under the given
\beta
, is a VC-subgraph class. This in turn implies stochastic equicontinuity.

section

4.2
is used to ensure normality of the limit distribution.

We verify all the conditions in
lemma

F.2
. Recall
I_{=}=\{i:{\beta^{*}_{i}}=1\}
is the set of active constraints. The local geometry of
B
at
\beta^{*}
is described by the
|I_{=}|
constraint functions
g_{i}(\beta)=e_{i}^{\top}\beta-1
,
i\in I_{=}
.

First, we verify the conditions on the probability distribution and the objective function.
A1 holds obviously for the map
\beta\mapsto\max_{i}\beta_{i}v_{i}(\theta)
.
A2 holds by
f\leq{\bar{v}}
.
A4 holds with Lipschitz constant
{\bar{v}}
.
A5 holds since by
section

2.3
there is a neighborhood
{N_{\text{diff}}}
of
\beta^{*}
such that for all
\beta\in{N_{\text{diff}}}
, the set
\{\theta:f(\theta,\cdot)\text{ not differentiable at $\beta$}\}
is measure zero.
A6 holds by
\|\nabla f(\theta,\beta)\|_{2}\leq n{\bar{v}}
.
B4 holds by
section

2.3
.

Second, we verify the conditions on the optimality.
B3 holds since the constraint functions are
g_{i}(\beta)=e_{i}^{\top}\beta-1
,
i\in I_{=}
, whose gradient vectors are obviously linear independent. Moreover, the set
\{\beta:\beta_{i}>0,i\in I_{=}\}
is nonempty.
B5 holds by
\nabla^{2}H(\beta^{*})=\nabla^{2}{\bar{f}}(\beta^{*})+{\operatorname*{diag}}(b_{i}/{\beta^{*}_{i}}^{2})\succcurlyeq{\operatorname*{diag}}(b_{i}/{\beta^{*}_{i}}^{2})
being positive definite.

Finally, we verify the stochastic equicontinuity condition.
Recall the definitions of the following two function classes from
lemma

F.16

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\{\theta\mapsto\max_{i}\{\beta_{1}v_{1}(\theta),\dots,\beta_{n}v_{n}(\theta)\}:\beta\in B\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\{\theta\mapsto D_{f}(\theta,\beta):\beta\in B\}.

Here
B=[0,1]^{n}
.
For any
\beta\in{N_{\text{diff}}}
we have
\nabla f(\cdot,\beta)=D_{f}(\cdot,\beta)\in{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
. In
lemma

F.16
we show that
{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
is VC-subgraph and Donsker. By
lemma

F.9
we know that a stochastic equicontinuity condition w.r.t. the
L^{2}
norm holds, i.e.,

\displaystyle\sup_{\beta\in[\delta_{t}]_{L^{2}}}\nu_{t}(D_{f}(\cdot,\beta)-D_{f}(\cdot,\beta^{*}))=o_{p}(1)

(65)

where

[\delta_{t}]_{L^{2}}=\{\beta:\beta\in{N_{\text{diff}}},\int\|D_{f}(\cdot,\beta)-D_{f}(\cdot,\beta^{*})\|_{2}^{2}s{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta}\leq\delta_{t}\}
,

\nu_{t}D_{f}=t^{-1/2}(P_{t}-P)D_{f}=t^{-1/2}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}(D_{f}({\theta^{\tau}})-\int D_{f}s{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta})
.
Next, we note for (almost every) fixed
\theta
,
\lim_{\beta\to\beta^{*}}\|D_{f}(\theta,\beta)-D_{f}(\theta,\beta^{*})\|^{2}=0
by
{\Theta_{\text{tie}}}(\beta^{*})
is measure zero (a condition implied by
section

2.3
).
Moreover, note

\displaystyle\lim_{\beta\to\beta^{*}}{\mathbb{E}}[\|D_{f}(\theta,\beta)-D_{f}(\theta,\beta^{*})\|_{2}^{2}]={\mathbb{E}}\big[\lim_{\beta\to\beta^{*}}\|D_{f}(\theta,\beta)-D_{f}(\theta,\beta^{*})\|_{2}^{2}\big]=0

where the exchange of limit and expectation is justified by bounded convergence theorem, and
by
lemma

F.14
, we can replace
[\delta_{t}]_{L^{2}}
with
[\delta_{t}]=\{\beta:\beta\in{N_{\text{diff}}},\|\beta-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\leq\delta_{t}\}
in
eq.

65
. Finally, note
\nabla{\bar{f}}(\beta^{*})={\mathbb{E}}[D_{f}(\theta,\beta^{*})]
, and
if
H_{t}
is differentiable at
\beta\in{N_{\text{diff}}}
, then
\nabla f({\theta^{\tau}},\beta)=D_{f}({\theta^{\tau}},\beta)
for all
\tau\in[t]
. Then

\displaystyle\sup_{[\delta_{t}]\cap\{\nabla H_{t}(\beta)\text{ exists}\}}\frac{\|(\nabla H_{t}-\nabla H)(\beta)-(\nabla H_{t}-\nabla H)(\beta^{*})\|_{2}}{t^{-1/2}+\|\beta-\beta^{*}\|_{2}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{[\delta_{t}]\cap\{\nabla H_{t}(\beta)\text{ exists}\}}\frac{\|(P_{t}-P)D_{f}(\cdot,\beta)-(P_{t}-P)D_{f}(\cdot,\beta^{*})\|_{2}}{t^{-1/2}+\|\beta-\beta^{*}\|_{2}}

(66)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{[\delta_{t}]}\sqrt{t}\|(P_{t}-P)D_{f}(\cdot,\beta)-(P_{t}-P)D_{f}(\cdot,\beta^{*})\|_{2}=o_{p}(1)

(by
eq.

65
)

and thus the required stochastic equicontinuity condition holds.

Now we are ready to invoke
lemma

F.2
. We need to find the
three objects,
C,q,\zeta_{t}
as in the lemma that characterize the limit distribution.
The critical cone
C
is

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=\{w\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}:w^{\top}e_{i}=0\text{ if $i\in I_{=}$ and ${\delta^{*}_{i}}>0$},\quad w^{\top}e_{i}\leq 0\text{ if $i\in I_{=}$ and ${\delta^{*}_{i}}=0$}\}

\displaystyle=\{w:Aw=0\}

(
section

4.2
)

where
A\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|I_{=}|\times n}
whose rows are
\{e_{i}^{\top},i\in I_{=}\}
. From here we can see the role of
section

4.2
is to ensure the
critical cone is a hyperplane, which ensures asymptotic normality of
\beta^{\gamma}
.

If
|I_{=}|=0
, i.e.,
\beta^{*}
lies in the interior of
B
, then
{\mathcal{P}}
is identity matrix, and the limit distribution is ture.

Now assume
|I_{=}|\geq 1
.
Note
AA^{\top}
is an identity matrix of size
{|I_{=}|}
and
A^{\top}A={\operatorname*{diag}}({\mathds{1}}(i\in I_{=}))={\operatorname*{diag}}({\mathds{1}}({\beta^{*}_{i}}=1))
.
The optimal Lagrangian multiplier is unique and so the piecewise quadratic function
q
is
q(w)=w^{\top}{\mathcal{H}}w
. Finally, the gradient error term is

\displaystyle\zeta_{t}=\frac{1}{t}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}\big(\mu^{\tau}-{\bar{\mu}^{*}}\big).

(67)

The unique minimizer of
w\mapsto\frac{1}{2}w^{\top}{\mathcal{H}}w+\zeta w
over
\{w:Aw=0\}
is
-({\mathcal{P}}{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{P}})^{\dagger}\zeta
where

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}=I_{n}-A^{\top}(AA^{\top})^{\dagger}A={\operatorname*{diag}}({\mathds{1}}(i\in{I_{<}}))={\operatorname*{diag}}({\mathds{1}}({\beta^{*}_{i}}<1)).

For completeness, we provide details for solving this quadratic problem.
By writing down the KKT conditions, the optimality condition is

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}{\mathcal{H}}&A^{\top}\\
A&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}w\\
\lambda\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}-\zeta\\
0\end{bmatrix}\implies\begin{bmatrix}w\\
\lambda\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}-({\mathcal{H}}^{-1}-{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}A^{\top}(A{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}A^{\top})^{-1}A{\mathcal{H}}^{-1})\zeta\\
-((A{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}A^{\top})^{-1}A{\mathcal{H}}^{-1})\zeta\end{bmatrix}

where
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|I_{=}|}
is the Lagrangian multiplier.
By a matrix equality, for any symmetric positive definite
{\mathcal{H}}
of size
n
and
A\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|I_{=}|\times n}
of rank
|I_{=}|
, it holds

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}-{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}A^{\top}(A{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}A^{\top})^{-1}A{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}=P_{A}(P_{A}{\mathcal{H}}P_{A})^{\dagger}P_{A}=(P_{A}{\mathcal{H}}P_{A})^{\dagger}

(68)

with
P_{A}=I_{n}-A^{\top}(AA^{\top})^{\dagger}A
.
We conclude that the asymptotic expansion

\displaystyle\sqrt{t}(\beta^{\gamma}-\beta^{*})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}D_{\beta}({\theta^{\tau}})+o_{p}(1)

(69)

holds, where

D_{\beta}(\theta)=-({\mathcal{P}}{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{P}})^{\dagger}({\mu^{*}}-{\bar{\mu}^{*}}),

and that the asymptotic distribution of
\sqrt{t}(\beta^{\gamma}-\beta^{*})
is
{\mathcal{N}}(0,\Sigma_{\beta})
with
\Sigma_{\beta}={\mathbb{E}}[D_{\beta}D_{\beta}^{\top}]
. Note
{\mathbb{E}}[D_{\beta}]=0
.

Proof of
\beta^{\gamma}{\,\overset{{p}}{\to}\,}\beta^{*}
.
This follows from
lemma

F.2
.

Proof of Asymptotic Distribution for pacing multiplier
\beta
.
This follows from the above discussion.

Proof of Asymptotic Distribution for revenue
{\mathsf{{REV}}}
.

We use a stochastic equicontinuity argument.
Given the item sequence
{\gamma}=(\theta^{1},\theta^{2}\dots)
, define the (random) operator

\displaystyle\nu_{t}g=\sqrt{t}(P_{t}-P)g=\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}(g({\theta^{\tau}})-{\mathbb{E}}[g])\;.

where
g:\Theta\to{\mathbb{R}}
,
{\mathbb{E}}[g]=\int gs{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta}
.
Note
p^{*}(\theta)=\max_{i}{\beta^{*}_{i}}{v_{i}(\theta)}=f(\theta,\beta^{*})
,
{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{*}=Pf(\cdot,\beta^{*})
,

{p^{\tau}}=f({\theta^{\tau}},\beta^{\gamma})
and

{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{\gamma}=P_{t}f(\cdot,\beta^{\gamma})
we obtain the decomposition

\displaystyle\sqrt{t}({\mathsf{{REV}}}^{\gamma}-{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{*})=\underbrace{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}\big(f({\theta^{\tau}},\beta^{*})-{\bar{f}}(\beta^{*})\big)}_{=:{\mathrm{{I}}}_{t}}+\underbrace{\nu_{t}(f(\cdot,\beta^{\gamma})-f(\cdot,\beta^{*}))}_{=:{\mathrm{{II}}}_{t}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\sqrt{t}(\bar{f}(\beta^{\gamma})-\bar{f}(\beta^{*}))}_{=:{\mathrm{{III}}}_{t}}

For the term
{\mathrm{{I}}}_{t}
, it can be written as

{\mathrm{{I}}}_{t}=\nu_{t}(p^{*}(\cdot)-{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{*})
.
By the linear representation for
\beta^{\gamma}-\beta^{*}
in
eq.

69
, applying the delta method, we get the linear representation result

\displaystyle{\mathrm{{III}}}_{t}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}\nabla{\bar{f}}(\beta^{*})^{\top}D_{\beta}({\theta^{\tau}})+o_{p}(1)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}({\bar{\mu}^{*}})^{\top}D_{\beta}({\theta^{\tau}})+o_{p}(1)

We will show
{\mathrm{{II}}}_{t}=o_{p}(1)
. The difficulty lies in that the operator
\nu_{t}
and the pacing multiplier
\beta^{\gamma}
depend on the same batch of items. This can be handled with the stochastic equicontinuity argument. The desired claim
{\mathrm{{II}}}_{t}=o_{p}(1)
follows by verifying that the function class
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}=\{\theta\mapsto f(\cdot,\beta):\beta\in B\}
(same as that defined in
lemma

F.16
) is VC-subgraph and Donsker. This is true by
lemma

F.16
.
By
lemma

F.7
we know for any
\delta_{t}\downarrow 0
,

\displaystyle\sup_{w\in[\delta_{t}]_{L^{2}}}\nu_{t}(f(\cdot,w)-f(\cdot,\beta^{*}))=o_{p}(1)

(70)

where
[\delta_{t}]_{L^{2}}=\{\beta:\beta\in B,\int(f(\cdot,\beta)-f(\cdot,\beta^{*}))^{2}s{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta}\leq\delta_{t}\}
. Noting that for all
\beta,w,\theta
, it holds
|f(\theta,\beta)-f(\theta,w)|\leq{\bar{v}}\|\beta-w\|_{\infty}
, we know that
\int\left[f(\cdot,\beta)-f\left(\cdot,\beta^{*}\right)\right]^{2}s{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta}\rightarrow 0
as
\beta\rightarrow\beta^{*}
. Then by
lemma

F.14
, we know
eq.

70
holds with
[\delta_{t}]_{L^{2}}
replaced with
[\delta_{t}]=\{\beta:\beta\in B,\|\beta-\beta^{*}\|_{2}\leq\delta_{t}\}
. Combined with the fact that
\beta^{\gamma}{\,\overset{{p}}{\to}\,}\beta^{*}
, by
lemma

F.15
we know
{\mathrm{{II}}}_{t}=o_{p}(1)
.

To summarize, we obtain the linear expansion

\displaystyle\sqrt{t}({\mathsf{{REV}}}^{\gamma}-{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{*})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}(p^{*}({\theta^{\tau}})-{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{*}+({\bar{\mu}^{*}})^{\top}D_{\beta}({\theta^{\tau}}))+o_{p}(1).

(71)

Let
D_{\mathsf{{REV}}}(\theta)=p^{*}({\theta^{\tau}})-{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{*}+({\bar{\mu}^{*}})^{\top}D_{\beta}({\theta^{\tau}})
.

We complete the proof of
theorem

4.3
.

G.4
Proof of
corollary

4.4

Proof G.3

(Proof of
corollary

4.4
)

For
i
such that
{\beta^{*}_{i}}=1
, we know
\beta^{\gamma}_{i}-1=o_{p}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}})
holds. Then

{\mathbb{P}}(\beta^{\gamma}_{i}<1-\varepsilon_{t})={\mathbb{P}}(o_{p}(1)>\sqrt{t}\varepsilon_{t})\to 0,

using the smoothing rate condition
\sqrt{t}\varepsilon_{t}\to c\in(0,\infty]
.
For
i
such that
{\beta^{*}_{i}}<1
, we know
\beta^{\gamma}-{\beta^{*}_{i}}=o_{p}(1)
by consistency of
\beta^{\gamma}
. Then

{\mathbb{P}}(\beta^{\gamma}_{i}<1-\varepsilon_{t})={\mathbb{P}}(o_{p}(1)<(1-{\beta^{*}_{i}})-\varepsilon_{t})\to 1

by
\varepsilon_{t}=o(1)
and
1-{\beta^{*}_{i}}>0
.

G.5
Proof of Normality of NSW,
u
, and
\delta
in FPPE

We define NSW and utility in limit FPPE.
We use
(.)^{*}
to denote limit FPPE quantities.
Given an FPPE
(\beta^{*},p^{*})
, we define by

\displaystyle{\delta^{*}_{i}}=b_{i}-\int p^{*}s{x^{*}_{i}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta}\;,\quad{\bar{\mu}^{*}_{i}}=\int v_{i}s{x^{*}_{i}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta}\;,

\displaystyle{u^{*}_{i}}={\bar{\mu}^{*}_{i}}+{\delta^{*}_{i}}\;.

(72)

the
leftover budget
, the
item utility
and the
total utility
of buyer
i
.
Let
{\delta^{*}},{\bar{\mu}^{*}},u^{*}
be the vectors that collect these quantities for all buyers.
In FPPE it holds that

\displaystyle{u^{*}_{i}}=b_{i}/{\beta^{*}_{i}}.

We next define these quantities in finite FPPE.
To emphasize dependence on the item sequence
\gamma
, we use
(.)^{\gamma}
to denote equilibrium quantities in
{\widehat{\textsf{{FPPE}}}}(b,v,1/t,\gamma)
.
We let
(\beta^{\gamma},{p^{\gamma}})
be an observed FPPE with
x^{\gamma}=(x^{\gamma}_{1},\dots)
.
The leftover budget
