\delta^{\gamma}_{i}=b_{i}-\sigma\sum_{\tau}{p^{\tau}}{x^{\gamma,\tau}_{i}}
, item utility
\bar{\mu}_{i}=\sigma{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}{v_{i}^{\tau}}{x^{\gamma,\tau}_{i}}
and total utility
u^{\gamma}_{i}=\delta^{\gamma}_{i}+\bar{\mu}_{i}
are defined similarly. Let
\delta^{\gamma},\bar{\mu},u^{\gamma}
be the vectors that collect these quantities for all buyers. The observed revenue is
{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{\gamma}=\sigma{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}{p^{\tau}}
, and NSW is
{\mathsf{{NSW}}}^{\gamma}={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}b_{i}\log u^{\gamma}_{i}
.

Corollary G.4

Under the same conditions as
theorem

4.3
,

\sqrt{t}(u^{\gamma}-u^{*})
,
\sqrt{t}(\delta^{\gamma}-{\delta^{*}})
and
\sqrt{t}({\mathsf{{NSW}}}^{\gamma}-{\mathsf{{NSW}}}^{*})

are asymptotically normal with
(co)variances

\Sigma_{u}=\allowbreak{\operatorname*{diag}}{({b_{i}}/({{\beta^{*}_{i}}})^{2})}\allowbreak\Sigma_{\beta}\allowbreak{\operatorname*{diag}}({b_{i}}/({{\beta^{*}_{i}}})^{2})
,

\Sigma_{\delta}=\allowbreak(I_{n}-{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{H}}_{B})^{\dagger})\allowbreak\Omega\allowbreak(I_{n}-{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{H}}_{B})^{\dagger})^{\top}
, and

\sigma^{2}_{\mathsf{{NSW}}}={\operatorname*{Vec}}(b_{i}/{\beta^{*}_{i}})^{\top}\allowbreak\Sigma_{\beta}\allowbreak{\operatorname*{Vec}}(b_{i}/{\beta^{*}_{i}})
, respectively.

Proof G.5

(Proof of
corollary

G.4
)

Proof of Asymptotic Distribution for individual utility
u
.
We use the delta method; see Theorem 3.1 from
Van der Vaart 2000
. Note
u^{*}=g(\beta^{*})
with
g:{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\to{\mathbb{R}^{n}},g(\beta)=[b_{1}/\beta_{1},\dots,b_{n}/\beta_{n}]^{\top}
. By
eq.

69
, it holds

\displaystyle\sqrt{t}(u^{\gamma}-u^{*})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}\nabla g(\beta^{*})D_{\beta}({\theta^{\tau}})+o_{p}(1).

(73)

Finally, noting
\nabla g(\beta^{*})={\operatorname*{diag}}(-b_{i}/({\beta^{*}_{i}})^{2})
we complete the proof.

Proof of Asymptotic Distribution for Nash social welfare
{\mathsf{{NSW}}}
.
We use the delta method. Note
{\mathsf{{NSW}}}^{*}=g(\beta^{*})
with
g:{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\to{\mathbb{R}},g(\beta)={\sum_{i=1}^{n}}b_{i}\log(b_{i}/\beta_{i})
. By
eq.

69
it holds

\displaystyle\sqrt{t}({\mathsf{{NSW}}}^{\gamma}-{\mathsf{{NSW}}}^{*})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}\nabla g(\beta^{*})^{\top}D_{\beta}({\theta^{\tau}})+o_{p}(1).

(74)

Finally, noting
\nabla g(\beta^{*})={\operatorname*{Vec}}(b_{i}/{\beta^{*}_{i}})
.

Proof of Asymptotic Distribution for leftover budget
\delta
.

This is a direct consequence of Theorem 4.1 in
Shapiro 1989
. By that theorem, it holds that

\displaystyle\sqrt{t}\begin{bmatrix}\beta^{\gamma}-\beta^{*}\\
\delta^{\gamma}_{I_{=}}-\delta^{*}_{I_{=}}\end{bmatrix}{\,\overset{{\,d\,\,}}{\rightarrow}\,}{\mathcal{N}}(0,\Sigma_{\text{joint}})

with

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\text{joint}}=\begin{bmatrix}{\mathcal{H}}&A^{\top}\\
A&0\end{bmatrix}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\Omega&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathcal{H}}&A^{\top}\\
A&0\end{bmatrix}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}({\mathcal{H}}_{B})^{\dagger}\Omega({\mathcal{H}}_{B})^{\dagger}&[Q\Omega({\mathcal{H}}_{B})^{\dagger}]^{\top}\\
Q\Omega({\mathcal{H}}_{B})^{\dagger}&Q\Omega Q^{\top}\end{bmatrix}

where
A\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|I_{=}|\times n}
whose rows are
\{e_{i}^{\top},i\in I_{=}\}
,
Q=(A{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}A^{\top})^{-1}A{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|I_{=}|\times n}
and
\Omega={\operatorname{Cov}}({\mu^{*}})
.
By a matrix equality, noting matrix
A
’s rows are distinct basis vectors, it holds

\displaystyle(A{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}A^{\top})^{-1}A{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}=A(I_{n}-{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{H}}_{B})^{\dagger})

Moreover, for other entries of
\delta^{\gamma}
, i.e.,
\delta^{\gamma}_{{I_{<}}}\,
, their asymptotic variance will be zero.
The matrix
(I_{n}-{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{H}}_{B})^{\dagger})\Omega(I_{n}-{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{H}}_{B})^{\dagger})^{\top}
is zero at the
(i,j)
-th entry if
i
or
j\in{I_{<}}
.
Summarizing, the asymptotic variance of
\sqrt{t}(\delta^{\gamma}-{\delta^{*}})
is
(I_{n}-{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{H}}_{B})^{\dagger})\Omega(I_{n}-{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{H}}_{B})^{\dagger})^{\top}
.

An alternative proof is by the delta method and a stochastic equicontinuity argument. To summarize, it holds
\sqrt{t}({\delta^{\gamma}-{\delta^{*}}}){\,\overset{{\,d\,\,}}{\rightarrow}\,}{\mathcal{N}}(0,(I_{n}-{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{H}}_{B})^{\dagger})\Omega(I_{n}-{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{H}}_{B})^{\dagger})^{\top})
and the linear expansion

\displaystyle\sqrt{t}(\delta^{\gamma}-{\delta^{*}})=t^{-1/2}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}(I_{n}-{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{H}}_{B})^{\dagger})({\mu^{*}}({\theta^{\tau}})-{\bar{\mu}^{*}})+o_{p}(1)

(75)

holds. In the case where
I_{=}=\emptyset
, i.e.,
{\delta^{*}_{i}}=0
for all
i
, we have
{\mathcal{H}}_{B}={\mathcal{H}}
and so
I_{n}-{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{H}}_{B})^{\dagger}=0
.

We complete the proof of
corollary

G.4

G.6
Proof of Fast Convergence of Pacing Multipliers

Lemma G.6

Let
section

2.3
and
section

4.2
hold. Then
{\mathbb{P}}(\beta^{\gamma}_{i}=1)\to 1
for all
i\in I_{=}
, and
{\mathbb{P}}({\delta^{\gamma}_{i}}=0)\to 1
for all
i\in I_{<}
.

Proof G.7

(Proof of
lemma

G.6
)

eq.

75
implies
\delta^{\gamma}_{i}-{\delta^{*}_{i}}=o_{p}(1)
for all
i\in I_{=}
.
section

4.2
implies
{\delta^{*}_{i}}>0
for all
i\in I_{=}
. Combining the two gives
{\mathbb{P}}({\delta^{\gamma}_{i}}>0,\forall i\in I_{=})\to 1
, which implies the desired claim by the strict complementarity between
\beta^{\gamma}\leq 1
and
\delta^{\gamma}\geq 0
.
The proof of
{\mathbb{P}}({\delta^{\gamma}_{i}}=0)\to 1
follows similarly.

G.7
Proof of
theorem

4.5

Proof G.8

(Proof of
theorem

4.5
)

According to the discussion in
section

D.5
,
we calculate the derivative of the map
\alpha\mapsto{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{*}_{\alpha,g}
at
\alpha=0
.

For a given perturbation
{(\alpha,g)}
, we let
p^{*}_{\alpha,g}
and
{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{*}_{\alpha,g}
be the limit FPPE price and revenue under supply distribution
s_{\alpha,g}
.
Let
S_{\alpha,g}(\theta)=\nabla_{\alpha}\log s_{\alpha,g}(\theta)
be the score function.
So
\nabla_{\alpha}s_{\alpha,g}=s_{\alpha,g}S_{\alpha,g}

and
\int S_{\alpha,g}s_{\alpha,g}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta=0
.
Obviously with our parametrization of
s_{\alpha,g}
we have
S_{0,g}(\theta)=g(\theta)
by
eq.

13
.
We next find the derivative of
\alpha\mapsto{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{*}_{\alpha,g}
at
\alpha=0
.
Recall
f
is defined as
f(\theta,\beta)=\max_{i}{v_{i}(\theta)}\beta_{i}
and the price is produced by the highest bid, i.e.,
p^{*}_{\alpha,g}(\theta)=\max_{i}\beta^{*}_{\alpha,g}{v_{i}(\theta)}=f(\theta,\beta^{*}_{\alpha,g})
.

\displaystyle\nabla_{\alpha}{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{*}_{\alpha,g}=\nabla_{\alpha}\int f(\theta,\beta^{*}_{\alpha,g})s_{\alpha,g}(\theta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle=\int[\nabla_{\beta}f(\theta,\beta^{*}_{\alpha,g})\nabla_{\alpha}\beta^{*}_{\alpha,g}+f(\theta,\beta^{*}_{\alpha,g})S_{\alpha,g}(\theta)]s_{\alpha,g}(\theta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\;.

Above we exchange the gradient and the expectation and then apply the chain rule.
By a perturbation result by Lemma 8.1 and Prop. 1 from
Duchi and Ruan 2021
, under
section

2.3
and
section

4.2
,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\alpha}\beta^{*}_{\alpha,g}|_{\alpha=0}=-({\mathcal{H}}_{B})^{\dagger}\Sigma_{{\mu^{*}},g}

with

\Sigma_{{\mu^{*}},g}={\mathbb{E}}_{s}[({\mu^{*}}(\theta)-{\bar{\mu}^{*}})g(\theta)^{\top}]
.
Plugging in
{\mathbb{E}}_{s}[\nabla_{\beta}f(\theta,\beta^{*}_{0,g})]={\bar{\mu}^{*}}
,
f(\theta,\beta^{*}_{0,g})=p^{*}(\theta)
and
S_{0,g}=g
,
we obtain

\displaystyle\nabla_{\alpha}{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{*}_{\alpha,g}|_{\alpha=0}

\displaystyle=-({\bar{\mu}^{*}})^{\top}({\mathcal{H}}_{B})^{\dagger}\Sigma_{{\mu^{*}},g}+{\mathbb{E}}_{s}[(p^{*}(\theta)-{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{*})g(\theta)]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\big[\big(-({\bar{\mu}^{*}})^{\top}({\mathcal{H}}_{B})^{\dagger}({\mu^{*}}(\theta)-{\bar{\mu}^{*}})+(p^{*}(\theta)-{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{*})\big)g(\theta)\big]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}[D_{\mathsf{{REV}}}(\theta)g(\theta)].

Now we have the two components required to invoke the local minimax result.
Given a symmetric quasi-convex loss
L:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, we recall the local asymptotic risk for any procedure
\{\hat{r}_{t}:\Theta^{t}\to{\mathbb{R}}\}
that aims to estimate the revenue:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{{LAR}}}_{\mathsf{{REV}}}(\{\hat{r}_{t}\})=

\displaystyle\sup_{g\in G_{d},d\in\mathbb{N}}\lim_{c\to\infty}\liminf_{t\to\infty}\sup_{\|\alpha\|_{2}\leq\frac{c}{\sqrt{t}}}{\mathbb{E}}_{s_{\alpha,g}^{\otimes t}}[L(\sqrt{t}(\hat{r}_{t}-{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{*}_{\alpha,g}))]\;.

Following the argument in
Duchi and Ruan 2021
it holds

\displaystyle\inf_{\{\hat{r}_{t}\}}{\mathsf{{LAR}}}_{\mathsf{{REV}}}(\{\hat{r}_{t}\})\geq{\mathbb{E}}[L({\mathcal{N}}(0,{\mathbb{E}}[D_{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{2}(\theta)]))]\;.

We complete the proof of
theorem

4.5
.

G.8
Proof of
theorem

4.6

Proof G.9

(Proof of
theorem

4.6
)

Proof of
\hat{\Sigma}_{\beta}{\,\overset{{p}}{\to}\,}\Sigma_{\beta}
.

We first show
\hat{\mathcal{H}}{\,\overset{{p}}{\to}\,}{\mathcal{H}}
by verifying conditions in
lemma

F.5
.
All conditions are easy to verify except the stochastic equicontinuity condition. By
lemma

F.16
we know the SE condition holds. We conclude
\hat{\mathcal{H}}{\,\overset{{p}}{\to}\,}{\mathcal{H}}
.
Next we show
{\mathbb{P}}(\hat{\mathcal{P}}={\mathcal{P}})\to 1
.
This follows from
corollary

4.4
.
We conclude
{\mathbb{P}}(\hat{\mathcal{P}}={\mathcal{P}})\to 1
.
We now show
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}\hat{\mathcal{H}}\hat{\mathcal{P}})^{\dagger}{\,\overset{{p}}{\to}\,}({\mathcal{P}}{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{P}})^{\dagger}
.
Wlog let
I_{=}=[k]
, and
I_{<}=[k+1,\dots,n]
.
For any
\epsilon>0
,

{\mathbb{P}}(\|(\hat{\mathcal{P}}\hat{\mathcal{H}}\hat{\mathcal{P}})^{\dagger}-({\mathcal{P}}{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{P}})^{\dagger}\|_{F}>{\epsilon})\leq{\mathbb{P}}(\|(\hat{\mathcal{P}}\hat{\mathcal{H}}\hat{\mathcal{P}})^{\dagger}-({\mathcal{P}}{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{P}})^{\dagger}\|_{F}>{\epsilon},\hat{\mathcal{P}}={\mathcal{P}})+{\mathbb{P}}(\hat{\mathcal{P}}\neq{\mathcal{P}})={\mathbb{P}}(\|[\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{<}]^{-1}-[{\mathcal{H}}_{<}]^{-1}\|_{F}>{\epsilon})+{\mathbb{P}}(\hat{\mathcal{P}}\neq{\mathcal{P}})\to 0

by
\hat{\mathcal{H}}{\,\overset{{p}}{\to}\,}{\mathcal{H}}
.
Next we show
{\operatorname{Cov}}({\mu^{*}})={\operatorname{Cov}}(\nabla f(\theta,\beta^{*}))
can be consistently estimated by

\frac{1}{t}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}(\mu^{\tau}-\bar{\mu})(\mu^{\tau}-\bar{\mu})^{\top}
,

\mu^{\tau}=[x_{1}^{\tau}v_{1}^{\tau},\dots,x_{n}^{\tau}v_{n}^{\tau}]^{\top}
, and
\bar{\mu}=\frac{1}{t}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}\mu^{\tau}
.
This follows by
lemma

C.5
.
We rewrite
\hat{\Sigma}_{\beta}
as

\hat{\Sigma}_{\beta}=(\hat{\mathcal{P}}\hat{\mathcal{H}}\hat{\mathcal{P}})^{\dagger}(\frac{1}{t}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}(\mu^{\tau}-\bar{\mu})^{\otimes 2})(\hat{\mathcal{P}}\hat{\mathcal{H}}\hat{\mathcal{P}})^{\dagger}

which
converges in probability to

({\mathcal{P}}{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{P}})^{\dagger}{\operatorname{Cov}}({\mu^{*}})({\mathcal{P}}{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{P}})^{\dagger}=\Sigma_{\beta}
.

Proof of
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{\mathsf{{REV}}}{\,\overset{{p}}{\to}\,}\sigma^{2}_{\mathsf{{REV}}}
.

We rewrite

\sigma^{2}_{\mathsf{{REV}}}=\underbrace{{\mathbb{E}}[(p^{*}-{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{*})^{2}]}_{{\mathrm{{I}}}_{t}}+\allowbreak\underbrace{({\bar{\mu}^{*}})^{\top}({\mathcal{P}}{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{P}})^{\dagger}{\operatorname{Cov}}({\mu^{*}})({\mathcal{P}}{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{P}})^{\dagger}{\bar{\mu}^{*}}}_{{\mathrm{{II}}}_{t}}+\allowbreak\underbrace{2{\mathbb{E}}[(p^{*}-{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{*})({\mu^{*}}-{\bar{\mu}^{*}})]^{\top}({\mathcal{P}}{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{P}})^{\dagger}{\bar{\mu}^{*}}}_{{\mathrm{{III}}}_{t}}

and

\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{\mathsf{{REV}}}=\allowbreak\underbrace{\frac{1}{t}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}({p^{\tau}}-{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{\gamma})^{2}}_{\hat{\mathrm{{I}}}_{t}}+\underbrace{({\bar{\mu}^{\gamma}})^{\top}(\hat{\mathcal{P}}\hat{\mathcal{H}}\hat{\mathcal{P}})^{\dagger}\hat{\Omega}(\hat{\mathcal{P}}\hat{\mathcal{H}}\hat{\mathcal{P}})^{\dagger}{\bar{\mu}^{\gamma}}}_{\hat{\mathrm{{II}}}_{t}}+\allowbreak\underbrace{2\bigg(\frac{1}{t}{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}}({p^{\tau}}-{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{\gamma})(\mu^{\tau}-{\bar{\mu}^{\gamma}})\bigg)^{\top}(\hat{\mathcal{P}}\hat{\mathcal{H}}\hat{\mathcal{P}})^{\dagger}{\bar{\mu}^{\gamma}}}_{\hat{\mathrm{{III}}}_{t}}\;.

We have
\hat{\mathrm{{I}}}_{t}{\,\overset{{p}}{\to}\,}{\mathrm{{I}}}_{t}
by invoking
lemma

F.16
, applying a uniform LLN and using the fact that
\beta^{\gamma}{\,\overset{{p}}{\to}\,}\beta^{*}
. And
\hat{\mathrm{{II}}}_{t}{\,\overset{{p}}{\to}\,}{\mathrm{{II}}}
holds by

\bar{\mu}{\,\overset{{p}}{\to}\,}{\bar{\mu}^{*}}
,
(\hat{\mathcal{P}}\hat{\mathcal{H}}\hat{\mathcal{P}})^{\dagger}{\,\overset{{p}}{\to}\,}({\mathcal{P}}{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{P}})^{\dagger}
and
\hat{\Omega}{\,\overset{{p}}{\to}\,}\Omega
, and applying
Slutsky’s theorem.
Finally,
\hat{\mathrm{{III}}}_{t}{\,\overset{{p}}{\to}\,}{\mathrm{{III}}}
by
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cdot{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
is Donsker by
lemma

F.13
and thus a uniform law of large number holds, and that
\beta^{\gamma}{\,\overset{{p}}{\to}\,}\beta^{*}
.

We complete the proof of
theorem

4.6
.

G.9
Proof of Simplified Estimation Results

Proof G.10

(Proof of
theorem

4.7
)

First we show
D_{\mathsf{{REV}}}(\theta)=p^{*}(\theta)-{\bar{\mu}^{*}}^{\top}({\mathcal{P}}{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{P}})^{\dagger}{\mu^{*}}(\theta)=\tilde{p}^{*}(\theta)
.
It suffices to show
(\beta^{*}-({\mathcal{P}}{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{P}})^{\dagger}{\bar{\mu}^{*}})^{\top}{\mu^{*}}(\theta)=\tilde{p}^{*}(\theta)
.
The cases of
I_{<}=\emptyset
or
I_{=}=\emptyset
can be handled easily. We assume both of them are nonempty. Also let
I_{=}=[k]
, and
I_{<}=[k+1,\dots,n]
without loss of generality.
The finite inverse moment assumption on
{\mathsf{{bidgap}}}
implies
\nabla^{2}{\mathbb{E}}[f(\theta,\beta^{*})]\beta^{*}=0
, and
{\mathcal{H}}={\operatorname*{diag}}(b_{i}/({\beta^{*}_{i}})^{2})
.
So
\beta^{*}=[1_{k};\beta^{*}_{<}]
where
\beta^{*}_{<}
is the subvector corresponding to
I_{<}
. Let
{\mathcal{H}}_{<}
be the lower-right matrix of
{\mathcal{H}}
corresponding to
I_{<}
.

lemma

2.5
,
eq.

17
and
\nabla^{2}{\mathbb{E}}[f(\theta,\beta^{*})]\beta^{*}=0
imply
({\mathcal{P}}{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{P}})^{\dagger}{\bar{\mu}^{*}}=[0_{k};\beta^{*}_{<}]
.
So
\beta^{*}-({\mathcal{P}}{\mathcal{H}}{\mathcal{P}})^{\dagger}{\bar{\mu}^{*}}=[1_{k};0_{n-k}]
. And
[1_{k};0_{n-k}]^{\top}{\mu^{*}}(\theta)=\sum_{i\in I_{=}}\mu^{*}_{i}(\theta)=\tilde{p}^{*}
.
The simplified formula for
\Sigma_{\beta}
follows easily.

Next we show
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{{\mathsf{{REV}}},\textup{sim}}{\,\overset{{p}}{\to}\,}\sigma^{2}_{\mathsf{{REV}}}
.
Suppose
I_{=}=\emptyset
, then
{\mathbb{P}}(\hat{I}_{=}=I_{=})\to 1
implies
\hat{\sigma}_{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{2}\to 0
.
Now suppose
I_{=}\neq\emptyset
.
In
lemma

F.16
we have shown both
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{3}
are VC-subgraph, and thus a uniform law of large number holds.
Since
{\mathbb{P}}(\hat{I}_{=}=I_{=})\to 1
, we analysis is carried out under the event
\hat{I}_{=}=I_{=}
.
Let
f(\theta,\beta)
be the function in
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
and
g
in
{\mathcal{F}}_{3}
. Let
h(\theta,\beta)=f(\theta,\beta)g(\theta,\beta)
.
Under the event
\hat{I}_{=}=I_{=}
, we have that
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{\mathsf{{REV}}}
is the sample variance of
h({\theta^{\tau}},\beta^{\gamma})
,
\tau\in[t]
, and
\hat{\sigma}_{\mathsf{{REV}}}^{2}
is the variance of the random variable
h(\theta,\beta^{*})
.
The consistency of the simplified revenue variance estimator follows by the uniform law of large number.
The consistency of the simplified estimator for
\Sigma_{\beta}
follows easily.

G.10
Proof of
theorem

4.8

Proof G.11

(Proof of
theorem

4.8
)

By the EG characterization of FPPE, we know that
\beta^{\gamma}(1)
, the pacing multiplier of the observed FPPE
{\widehat{\textsf{{FPPE}}}}\big(\pi b,v(1),\frac{\pi}{t_{1}},\gamma(1)\big)
, solves the following dual EG program

\displaystyle\min_{B}\frac{1}{t_{1}}\sum_{\tau=1}^{t_{1}}\max_{i}{v_{i}(\theta^{\tau})}\beta_{i}-{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}b_{i}\log(\beta_{i})

(76)

The major technical challenge is that the number of summands in the first summation is also random.
Given a fixed integer
k
and a sequence of items
(\theta^{1,1},\dots,\theta^{1,k})
, define

\displaystyle\beta^{\text{lin},k}(1)=\beta^{*}(1)+\frac{1}{{k}}\sum_{\tau=1}^{k}D_{\beta}(1,\theta^{1,\tau}),

\displaystyle\beta^{k}(1)=\text{the unique pacing multiplier in ${\widehat{\textsf{{FPPE}}}}(b,v(1),k^{-1},(\theta^{1,1},\dots,\theta^{1,k}))$}

Here
D_{\beta}(1,\cdot)=-({\mathcal{H}}_{B}(1))^{\dagger}({\mu^{*}}(1,\cdot)-{\bar{\mu}^{*}}(1))

where

{\mathcal{H}}_{B}(1),{\mu^{*}}(1,\cdot)
and
{\bar{\mu}^{*}}(1)
are the projected Hessian in
eq.

18
, item utility function in
eq.

15
, and total item utility vector in
eq.

16
in the limit market
{\textsf{{FPPE}}}(b,v(1),s)
.
Note
{\mathbb{E}}[D_{\beta}(1,\cdot)]=0
.
Note
\beta^{\gamma}(1)=\beta^{t_{1}}
since scaling the supply and the budget at the same time does not change the equilibrium pacing multiplier.
We introduce the following asymptotic equivalence results:

Lemma G.12

Recall
t_{1}\sim\text{Bin}(\pi,t)
. If
section

4.2
and
section

2.3
hold for the limit market
{\textsf{{FPPE}}}(b,v(1),s)
, then

•

\sqrt{t}(\beta^{\gamma}(1)-\beta^{\text{lin},t_{1}})=o_{p}(1)
as
t\to\infty
.

•

\sqrt{t}(\beta^{\text{lin},t_{1}}-\beta^{\text{lin},\lfloor\pi t\rfloor})=o_{p}(1)
as
t\to\infty
.

Here
\lfloor a\rfloor
is the greatest integer less than or equal to
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
A similar result holds for the market limit
{\textsf{{FPPE}}}(b,v(0),s)
and the influence function
D_{\beta}(0,\cdot)
is defined similarly.

With
lemma

G.12
, we write

\displaystyle\sqrt{t}(\hat{\tau}_{\beta}-\tau_{\beta})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{t}(\beta^{\gamma}(1)-\beta^{*}(1))-\sqrt{t}(\beta^{\gamma}(0)-\beta^{*}(0))

\displaystyle=\sqrt{t}\bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lfloor\pi t\rfloor}}\sum_{\tau=1}^{\lfloor\pi t\rfloor}D_{\beta}(1,\theta^{1,\tau})-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lfloor(1-\pi)t\rfloor}}\sum_{\tau=1}^{\lfloor(1-\pi)t\rfloor}D_{\beta}(0,\theta^{0,\tau})\bigg)+o_{p}(1)

(
lemma

G.12
)

\displaystyle{\,\overset{{\,d\,\,}}{\rightarrow}\,}{\mathcal{N}}\bigg(0,\frac{1}{\pi}{\operatorname{Var}}[D_{\beta}(1,\cdot)]+\frac{1}{{(1-\pi)}}{\operatorname{Var}}[D_{\beta}(0,\cdot)]\bigg).

(independence between
\{\theta^{1,\tau}\}_{\tau}
and
\{\theta^{0,\tau}\}_{\tau}
)

Proof of CLT for
\tau_{\beta}
.
It follows from the above discussion.

Proof of CLT for
\tau_{u}
.
We begin with the linear expansion
eq.

73
and repeat the same argument.

Proof of CLT for
\tau_{\mathsf{{REV}}}
.
We begin with the linear expansion
eq.

71
and repeat the same argument.

Proof of CLT for
\tau_{\mathsf{{NSW}}}
.
We begin with the linear expansion
eq.

74
and repeat the same argument.

We complete the proof of
theorem

4.8
.

In order to prove
lemma

G.12
, we will need the following lemma.

Lemma G.13

If
X_{t}=o_{p}(1)
and
T\sim\text{Bin}(\pi,t)
and
T
and the sequence are independent, then
X_{T}=o_{p}(1)
.

Proof G.14

(Proof of
lemma

G.13
)

By
X_{t}=o_{p}(1)
we know for all
{\epsilon}>0
it holds
{\mathbb{P}}(|X_{t}|>{\epsilon})\to 0
, or equivalently
\sup_{k\geq t}{\mathbb{P}}(|X_{k}|>{\epsilon})\to 0
as
t\to\infty
. By a concentration for binomial distribution, we know for all
\delta>0
, it holds
{\mathbb{P}}(|T-\pi t|>\delta\pi t)\leq 2\exp(-\delta^{2}\pi t/3)
. Now write

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(|X_{T}|>\epsilon)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{P}}(|X_{T}|>{\epsilon},T\in(1\pm\delta)\pi t)+{\mathbb{P}}(T\notin(1\pm\delta)\pi t)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\in(1\pm\delta)\pi t}{\mathbb{P}}(|X_{k}|>{\epsilon}){\mathbb{P}}(T=k)+2\exp(-\delta^{2}\pi t/3)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{k\geq(1-\delta)\pi t}{\mathbb{P}}(|X_{k}|>{\epsilon})+2\exp(-\delta^{2}\pi t/3)\to 0\text{ as $t\to\infty$}

where in the second inequality we use the independence between
T
and the sequence.
We conclude
X_{T}=o_{p}(1)
, completing proof of
lemma

G.13
.

Proof G.15

(Proof of
lemma

G.12
)

The first statement uses the independence between
t_{1}
and the items
(\theta^{1,1},\theta^{1,2},\dots)
.
Define
R(k)=\sqrt{t}(\beta^{k}(1)-\beta^{\text{lin},k}(1))
. By
eq.

69
, we have
R(k)=o_{p}(1)
as
k\to\infty
.
With this notation, the first statement is equivalent to
R(t_{1})=o_{p}(1)
where
t_{1}\sim\text{Bin}(\pi,t)
, which holds true by
lemma

G.13
.

The second statement is equivalent to
\sqrt{\lfloor\pi t\rfloor}\big(\beta^{\text{lin},t_{1}}(1)-\beta^{\text{lin},\lfloor\pi t\rfloor}(1)\big)=o_{p}(1)
.
To prove this we use a Komogorov’s inequality.
By Theorem 2.5.5 from
Durrett 2019
, for any
{\epsilon}>0
, (let
\sigma_{D_{\beta}}={\mathbb{E}}[\|D_{\beta}(1,\theta)\|_{2}^{2}]^{1/2}
)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\sqrt{\lfloor\pi t\rfloor}\sup_{(1-{\epsilon})\lfloor\pi t\rfloor\leq m\leq(1+{\epsilon})\lfloor\pi t\rfloor}\|\beta^{\text{lin},m}(1)-\beta^{\text{lin},(1-{\epsilon})\lfloor\pi t\rfloor}(1)\|_{2}\geq\delta\sigma_{D_{\beta}}\bigg)\leq\frac{2{\epsilon}}{\delta^{2}}.

Then

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\big(\sqrt{\lfloor\pi t\rfloor}\big\|\beta^{\text{lin},t_{1}}(1)-\beta^{\text{lin},\lfloor\pi t\rfloor}(1)\big\|_{2}\geq\delta\big)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{P}}\big(\sqrt{\lfloor\pi t\rfloor}\big\|\beta^{\text{lin},t_{1}}(1)-\beta^{\text{lin},\lfloor\pi t\rfloor}(1)\big\|_{2}\geq\delta,\;(1-{\epsilon})\lfloor\pi t\rfloor\leq t_{1}\leq(1+{\epsilon})\lfloor\pi t\rfloor\big)

\displaystyle\quad+{\mathbb{P}}\big(t_{1}\notin\big[(1-{\epsilon})\lfloor\pi t\rfloor,(1+{\epsilon})\lfloor\pi t\rfloor\big]\big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2{\epsilon}\sigma_{D_{\beta}}^{2}}{\delta^{2}}+{\mathbb{P}}\big(t_{1}\notin\big[(1-{\epsilon})\lfloor\pi t\rfloor,(1+{\epsilon})\lfloor\pi t\rfloor\big]\big)\to\frac{2{\epsilon}\sigma_{D_{\beta}}^{2}}{\delta^{2}}

Finally, since the above holds for all
\epsilon>0
, we obtain
\sqrt{\lfloor\pi t\rfloor}(\beta^{\text{lin},t_{1}}-\beta^{\text{lin},\lfloor\pi t\rfloor})=o_{p}(1)
.
We complete the proof of
lemma

G.12
.

Appendix H
Experiments

H.1
Hessian Estimation

Setup.

We look at the following configuration of markets and the smoothing parameter
d
.
Note we will be evaluating Hessian at a prespecified point and do not need to form any market equilibria in this experiment.
We consider
n=9
buyers. The item size
t
ranges from 200 to 5000, at a log scale.
Budget does not need to be specified.
Buyers’ values are drawn from uniform, exponential, or truncated standard normal distributions.
The smoothing parameter
d
is chosen from the grid [0.10, 0.17, 0.25, 0.32, 0.40, 0.47, 0.55, 0.62, 0.70].
We evaluate the Hessian
\nabla^{2}H
at a pre-specified point
\beta=[0.200,0.333,0.467,0.600,0.733,0.867,1.000]
, and plot the estimated diagonal values,
\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{ii}
for
i\in[7]
, against the number of items
t
.
Under each configuration we repeat for 10 trials.

Results.

See

figs.

8
,
9
and
10
.
We see that
d
represents a bias-variance trade-off. For a small
d
(0.10, 0.17, 0.25), the variance of the estimated value
\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{ii}
is small and yet bias is large (since the plots seem to be trending to some point as number of item increases). For a large
d
(0.55, 0.62, 0.70) variance is large and yet the bias is small (the estimates are stationary around some point). It is suggested to use
d\in(0.32,0.47)
.

Figure 8:
Effect of smoothing parameter on numerical difference estimation of Hessian. Each curve represents the estimated value of
{\mathcal{H}}_{ii}
. Uniform values.

Figure 9:
Effect of smoothing parameter on numerical difference estimation of Hessian. Each curve represents the estimated value of
{\mathcal{H}}_{ii}
. Exponential values.

Figure 10:
Effect of smoothing parameter on numerical difference estimation of Hessian. Each curve represents the estimated value of
{\mathcal{H}}_{ii}
. Truncated normal values.

. Truncated normal values.
